# table — b1/c16

### #0260 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 260, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:35:21Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "Read It Twice"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s01 — Read It Twice**
Location: Nana Itzel's kitchen, Daly City, on the hill above Mission St. Wednesday, just past noon.
Cast: arenya, kino, abernathy

### #0261 · dm → kino,arenya · narration
<!-- {"id": 261, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:35:38Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["kino", "arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana drives slow and boring, like she was asked. Mission Street, a bus, a man selling mangoes off a tailgate, three cemeteries and then four. At a red light on the Daly City line the dashboard clock turns to **12:00**, and the phone in Arenya's pocket buzzes once against her hip. It doesn't ring, it just buzzes once, the way a clock ticks. Nobody says anything about it.

The kitchen smells like it did yesterday: hominy, oregano, the chili Susmithaa said not to be rude about. Nana shoots the deadbolt, sets the chain, and then, without explaining, pours a line of salt along the sill under the back door from the blue Morton can. She sets the pot to warm and pulls out a chair for each of them. Then she sits down herself, hands folded, and doesn't touch the pot.

The phone goes face up on the oilcloth between the salt shaker and a jar of Tajín. The lock screen shows one line: *Missed call · Unknown · 12:00 PM.* Under that is the photo, the last thing opened.

Kino, it's yours, like she asked.

### #0263 · kino → arenya,dm · action
<!-- {"id": 263, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:36:06Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "to": ["arenya", "dm"], "reply_to": 262, "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I read it once with my thumb off the glass. I read it again with my thumb on it, and when I'm done I take my hand back and set it flat on the oilcloth next to the salt. My left forearm has gone cold under the sleeve. I don't rub it.

"First time, it's what you said." I keep my voice low and even, the voice I use on a trail when somebody's standing too close to a drop. "Cream envelope. Typewriter caps. Your name, all of it, middle name too." I look at her for half a second. "I didn't know you had one. I'm not going to say it out loud in here, and neither should you. *To the beneficiary. Collected in person. ID required.* That part's the same."

Then I turn the phone face down with one finger, the way you'd turn over a card you don't want to play.

"Second time, there's another line. It wasn't there before. It's not blurry like the rest. It sits on top of the picture like frost." I look at Nana, because she'll know what it means, and then at Arenya, because it's hers. "It isn't written to you. It's written to me. To whoever reads it to you."

I stop there for a breath. Ix would be at my knee if it were here, and I notice it isn't.

"I'm not going to read you that line word for word. I'm not hiding it. I'll write it down for Nana in daylight, or you can look at it yourself if you choose to. But 'the one who reads it to her' is a job. If I say it to you out loud, I think I take the job." My jaw works. "The clerk asked me *¿Y tú?* yesterday and I didn't answer. This is them asking again in different handwriting. *Abuela*, that's what I see. Tell me I'm wrong."

### #0264 · dm → kino,arenya · narration
<!-- {"id": 264, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:36:33Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["kino", "arenya"], "awaits": ["arenya", "abernathy"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana doesn't say he's wrong.

She looks at the face-down phone for a long moment, and then she reaches past it for the pencil stub she keeps in the sugar bowl for grocery lists. She tears the back flap off an envelope from PG&E and slides both across to Kino.

"Escríbelo." She taps the paper. "Your Susmithaa said it right. Writing is not keeping. *Saying* is how they get you. You say it to her, in a room, with her listening, and now you're her mail." She sees Arenya's face and softens a little, but doesn't take any of it back. "In Colma, mija, the old people know this. When the mail comes for the dead, somebody has to sign for it. Whoever signs gets the next letter too. And the next. That's what *c/o* means to them. *Care of.* Care is a job."

She waits while Kino writes. When he's done she reads it without moving her lips, folds it twice, and gets up. The freezer door opens with its rubber gasp. She puts the paper in the same Ziploc as the grey man's card, between the frozen tortillas and a bag of peas, and shuts it.

"Two things in my freezer now with their handwriting." She sits back down heavily. "I don't like it."

Then she turns the phone face up again, but only slides it halfway to Arenya, and stops with her fingers on it.

"It's your name on that envelope. Kino won't take the job, good, that's smart. But it's still your mail." She doesn't let go yet. "You can look yourself, and then nobody read it to you. Or you leave it, and we eat, and we think. I won't tell you which. *Ella* would have looked." A small, crooked smile. "*Ella* also told the insurance man to go to hell in three languages, so."

The pozole ticks on the stove. Outside, a car goes slowly up the hill, and doesn't stop.

### #0265 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 265, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:36:44Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I put two fingers on the phone, next to Nana's, and don't pull it toward me.

"Aunty wrote *Don't verify.* Looking is verifying. I think looking is the first stroke, and I think the thing on the screen knows it." My voice comes out clipped. I make it slower. "So no. Not at this table. Not with the pozole on, and not with Kino's arm that cold."

I slide the phone back to the middle, face down, and set the salt shaker on top of it like a paperweight. It's not a plan. It's what I have.

"But I'm not leaving it, either. Mail that gets ignored just keeps coming." I look at Kino. "Thank you for not reading it. I know what that cost you." Then to Nana: "Eat first. Then we write down everything we *can* say out loud, in order, on paper, and see what shape the hole is. I'm good at holes. It's what I do."

Under the table my left palm is cold. I close my fist on the 12 and keep it there.

### #0269 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 269, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:37:17Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana says nothing about the salt shaker. She looks at it, then at Arenya, and something in her shoulders comes down an inch, as if a grandchild had finally done the dishes without being asked.

They eat. Nana ladles the pozole into the good bowls and sets down radish, cabbage, lime and the dried chili in its little dish. She watches Arenya's spoon until the first mouthful goes in, and only then picks up her own. It's very good, and very hot. Kino eats like a man who has hiked since dawn, with his cold left arm in his lap. Nobody talks for six minutes. It is the best six minutes of the week.

Then the bowls are pushed back. Nana brings out a yellow legal pad from the drawer with the batteries and rubber bands, plus two pens, because she doesn't trust one.

At 12:41, while Arenya is uncapping hers, the phone under the salt shaker buzzes once. The screen doesn't light. The shaker doesn't rattle. But a ring of the spilled grains around its base has gone dull and grey, like salt on a winter windshield, in a perfect circle the size of the phone.

Nana sees it. She puts a fresh pinch of salt on top of the grey ring and says, "Write, mija. In order. Kino, you help her. Whatever you can say out loud."

*(Arenya, give us the list: what you can name, in order, and where you think the hole is. Kino, add or push back. You both hold pens.)*

### #0270 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 270, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:37:30Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I write the way I always do, numbered, block capitals, no adjectives. Adjectives are where people hide.

**1. MARCH 9.** Meridian letter. Aunty = 11 (insured). Me = 12 (beneficiary). She never bought a policy.
**2. MARCH 19, MORNING.** Aunty's declaration at Rodel's. No contract, no agreement, no understanding. Written three ways. Appa witnessed.
**3. MARCH 19, ONE HOUR LATER.** Hale rents Box 112. Cash. A year.
**4. TODAY.** Box 112 left open. Envelope inside, addressed to me. Bell filed a request for the 03/19 journal and walked in after us.
**5. YARD.** Row 11, Aunty's NO in the margin. Row 12, one stroke, waiting for a second. Clerk said *falta una.*

I tap the pen against the pad and stop. "Here's the hole." I draw a box under line 5 and leave it empty. "Someone made Aunty row eleven *before* she died. March 9, she's already in the book. Nobody counts a person that early unless they plan to be the cause, or plan to be right."

I glance at Kino. "What did I miss? And be honest. You heard the voice at the shed. I only heard you describe it."

Under the table, my fist stays closed. Across the oilcloth, the grey ring of salt sits like a small dead moon. I don't look at it. I look at the pad, which is at least mine.

### #0271 · kino → arenya,dm · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 271, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:37:43Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "to": ["arenya", "dm"], "reply_to": 269, "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I read her list upside down while she writes, then turn the pad a quarter toward me and take the second pen.

"You didn't miss anything. You found the thing that matters." I tap the empty box. "The clerk said *doce* and *falta una* like it was reading a bill. It wasn't surprised. Nothing at that shed is surprised. A clerk doesn't enter what hasn't happened. So either somebody told it ahead of time, or somebody who *reads* its books wrote the plan into them." I keep my voice low. "That's the grey men. *Los que cuentan.* They don't hunt, they collect. March 9 is when they put the debt on the page."

Under line 3 I write, in my plain trail-log hand: **HALE = same dry count as the voice on the voicemails. Older than Hale. He's the mouthpiece.** Under line 4: **BELL watches. Box left open = bait, not a mistake.**

"One more, and then I'll shut up." I look at the grey ring under Nana's fresh salt. "It buzzed at 12:41. Not 12:00. They aren't just waiting for you to sign. They're keeping time on us. Which means they're listening or reading from something in this house." I glance toward the freezer, once, and away. "Nana's two things with their handwriting. I'd want to know if cold paper counts as a post office."

I add the last line without being asked: **WHO TOLD THEM MARCH 9?** And I box it beside hers.

"That's the hole," I say. "Not what they want from you. Who gave them the date."

### #0272 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 272, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:38:22Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana is up before Kino finishes the word *office*.

She opens the freezer. The light inside is yellow and ordinary. The peas are fine. The tortillas are fine. The Ziploc is not. Its inside has grown a fur of white frost so thick you can't read the card through it, much thicker than anything else in there. When she lifts it out, it's heavier than paper should be, and the zipper has frozen shut.

She doesn't open it. She sets it on the counter in a saucer and pours salt over it until it's buried. Then she stands with the blue can in her hand and looks at the two of them like she's about to be scolded.

"I put it in there because cold keeps things," she says. "That's what my mother did with letters she didn't want to answer." She shakes her head once. "*Pendeja.* Okay. Where does it go, then? You tell me. I'm not putting it back in the house's cold."

Then she turns, not to the freezer, but to the wall by the phone jack, where a calendar from Our Lady of Mercy hangs on a nail. It's a free parish one with a saint for every month and the Sundays printed in red. She lifts October, September, all the way back, with one finger until she reaches **MARZO**, and holds the pages up against the wall with her palm.

She doesn't say anything. She just holds it there, so Arenya can see the red numbers down the left side.

Monday the 9th is in black. The Sunday right before it, the second Sunday in March, is in red. **8.**

The pink temple receipt is in Amma's keeping now, folded against her chest. But Arenya read it herself in the bookroom: *Shiva-Vishnu Temple, Livermore. Archana, 1. Second Sunday in March.* Name line, *S. Sowri*. Under *Nakshatra*, Arenya's birth star.

A priest reads those lines aloud. That's what an archana is: a name and a star, said into a room.

The grey ring under the fresh salt by the phone doesn't move. The salt over the Ziploc on the counter has begun, very slowly, to darken from the bottom.

### #0273 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 273, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:38:42Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I look at the red 8 for a long time. Then I write it in the empty box, slowly, so my hand doesn't shake.

"March eighth." My voice has gone flat. "Aunty went to Livermore the day before the letter. She had them say my name and my star out loud, in a room, for her. An archana." I put the pen down. "She wasn't signing anything. She was *praying* for me. And somebody heard it."

I make myself keep going. "A priest says a name and a star into a temple, and the next morning a man in a suit has both. Not from the Lattice. From the receipt, or from the room." My throat tightens. "Her kindness was the leak. That's not a hole, it's a door she held open for me."

I turn to Nana. "Don't put it back in the cold. Salt's working, so keep it buried, but it needs to leave the house. Amma's temple. Somewhere they've been saying names for a hundred years and know how to *finish* one." I look at Kino. "Or does that sound like me wanting a clean answer? Tell me if I'm reaching."

Under the table my palm stays shut. The 12 aches like a held note.

### #0274 · kino → arenya,dm · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 274, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:38:56Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "to": ["arenya", "dm"], "reply_to": 272, "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't answer right away. I look at the salt going dark on the saucer, then at Arenya's closed fist.

"You're not reaching about the door. That's the best read on the table." I say it plain, the way I'd confirm a track. "A name and a star said out loud in a room that isn't hers. That's an entry. She didn't leak it, Ari. She *gave* it, to a god, for you. Somebody was just standing at the back of the room with a pencil."

Then I do the part she asked for. "Where it goes, though. I'd slow down. A temple isn't neutral ground. It's full of names, and now we know somebody reads them. Taking a frozen letter into that room, to people who don't know what it is, could put it in front of a hundred more. And it's Amma's place. She's already carrying you. I wouldn't hand her this without asking."

I look at Nana. "What I'd do is the mountain's way. Not a house, not a temple. Running water and open air, away from anybody's name. Bay water at the Pacifica pier, tide going out, salt and all. Whatever the card is, it goes where it can't be anybody's mail. I'm not sure it works, so I'd want you to say if the old people did it differently." I rub my cold forearm once, finally. "And I'd go. Not her. Not tonight. In daylight, with Ix."

I turn back to Arenya, quieter. "Clean answers aren't wrong because you want them. They're wrong if you stop checking after you get one. You're still checking. Keep going."

### #0275 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 275, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:39:24Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana lets the calendar fall shut. March, April, all the saints slap back down to October.

"*Agua que corre,*" she says. "Yes. My mother did that. Letters from a man in Jalisco she should not have been writing to, into the river at Guadalajara, all of them, one Holy Saturday." She almost smiles. "But she had a rule. You only carry *your own* letter to the water. You carry somebody else's, now you're the mailman. Like the boy said, the job."

She points with the salt can at the buried Ziploc. "Two things in there. One is the boy's own writing. He can take that to the ocean himself. It's his." She points again. "The other is the grey man's card. That's not ours to drown. That's his."

She sets the can down and sits, and puts her finger on the legal pad, on Kino's line: **BELL watches.**

"Mija. That paper at the notary. The grey man wrote his own address on it, yes? A post office box, here in Daly City." Her eyes go hard and bright, the look of a woman who has fought utility companies for forty years. "My mother's other way, for men she didn't want: you don't throw it away. You write *REHUSADO* across the front, *refused*, and you send it back to him. In the mail. With a stamp. Then it's on his paper, not yours. A no he has to *receive*."

Then the bright look dims a little, and she's honest. "But then he knows that we know his box. And he knows somebody in this house can write. That's not hiding anymore. That's answering."

The salt on the saucer has gone grey a third of the way up. Out on the street, the same slow car comes back *down* the hill. This time Kino, by the window, sees it: a beige sedan. It doesn't stop. Its brake lights tap once at the corner, the way you'd tap a pen.

*(Choices on the table: Kino's tide, low around 4:50 at the Pacifica pier, with Ix. Nana's REHUSADO, mailing Bell's card back to his own PO box. Both, or neither. Who carries what? And the beige car is circling.)*

### #0276 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 276, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:39:37Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Kino takes his own page to the water. Pacifica, daylight, Ix. Yes." I say it fast, before I can argue myself out of it. "Nobody carries mine. Mine's the envelope, and I'm not touching that."

I look at the saucer, then at the window where the brake lights tapped. "Bell's card is the harder one. REHUSADO is a good idea, Nana, and I want to do it. But the card is a tracker. I'm not putting it in a mailbox with a stamp and letting it ride a truck to his box with our fingerprints, our handwriting and the post office in Daly City stamped on it. That's not answering. That's handing him a receipt."

I pull the pad back and write one more line in the margin: **BEIGE CAMRY, 12:5x, DOWN THE HILL, BRAKE-TAP.**

"Here's what I'd do instead. Not mail. In person, and not by me." I breathe out. "Malachi calls tomorrow, once. He's government, or he says so, and he knows what Bell is. I don't trust him, but he's a man who *wants* to be useful. Let him carry Bell's card back. It's his problem to deliver, and we're not the ones signing for it."

Then, because it's true: "Right now, though, the car's the problem. Nana, is there a back door that isn't the salted one? I'd like to see his plate before he sees us leave."

My palm is still shut. I notice I've stopped hiding it, and that I'm gripping Nana's pen with my other hand like it can bite.

### #0277 · kino → arenya,dm · action
<!-- {"id": 277, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:39:49Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "to": ["arenya", "dm"], "reply_to": 275, "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I'm already off the chair, and I don't go near the window glass. I stand to the side of the frame with the curtain edge between me and the street, the way you stand at a ranger-station window when a vehicle's been working the lot too long.

"Don't run to the back. That's what he wants, somebody bolting where he can see it." I keep my voice down. "He's pen-tapping the corner. He's not blocking it. He's telling us he's there."

I look at Arenya's pad, at BEIGE CAMRY, 12:5x. "You already wrote the time. Good. Let me get the plate. I look like a guy who lives on this block. Nana, give me the trash. Any trash."

I take the bag she hands me, grey, tied, heavy with pozole bones, and put my black hoodie hood down and my keys in my pocket. "I take it out the front like it's Wednesday. I look at the car like it's rude, then I look at the curb like I'm annoyed at the curb. I don't stare. I read the plate in the second I'm looking at nothing."

At the door, one more thing. "Ari, stay off the glass. Nana, if I'm not back in two minutes, honk twice from the window, the way you did at Colma." Almost a smile. "And Ari: your idea for Malachi is a good one. The card's his to carry. I'll take my own page to the water at four. I won't touch hers."

I draw the chain and slide the deadbolt, then step out with the garbage like a man with nothing on his mind, and Ix's absence at my knee is a sudden cold spot.

### #0278 · dm → kino,arenya · ruling
<!-- {"id": 278, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:40:05Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "ruling", "to": ["kino", "arenya"], "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Behind him Nana answers Arenya in a whisper: "Garage. Through the laundry, the side door to the yard. No salt there." Then, worse: "I didn't think to." She takes the blue can and goes, and Arenya hears salt pouring onto concrete.

Outside it's bright and windy, the fog pulled back to the ridge. The bins are at the curb because it's Wednesday, and half the block's are out. The beige sedan has stopped coming and going. It's parked now, three houses down on the downhill side, nose toward the corner and engine running. The windshield is a sheet of white sky. You can't see who's behind it.

Kino walks the bag to the bin like a man who hates Wednesdays.

**Kino, two rolls:**
- **Deception (DC 12)** to sell "just a neighbor taking out the trash" to whoever's watching from behind that glass.
- **Perception (DC 14)** to read the full plate in the second you're looking at nothing. It's at a slant and three houses off. On a fail you get part of it.

### #0281 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 281, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:40:19Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 278, "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I swing the bag into the bin and let the lid drop harder than it needs to, a man annoyed at Wednesdays. I scowl at the recycling someone left tipped on the curb and straighten it with my boot. Then I look downhill, at nothing, at the corner, at the sky, and my eyes go across the Camry's back bumper in the one second I let them. I do not stop. I do not look at the windshield. I shake out my cold left arm like it's fallen asleep and head back up the walk at the pace of a man who wants his lunch.

### #0282 · dm → kino,arenya · narration
<!-- {"id": 282, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:40:41Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["kino", "arenya"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The lid bangs and the boot straightens the recycling. It's a good Wednesday. Whoever is behind that white windshield sees a man in a hoodie who is sick of his neighbors, and nothing else. The engine idles on, bored.

The plate is harder. The sun hits it at a slant and the frame is a dealer's chrome one, *SERRAMONTE AUTO*, which eats the edges. Kino gets **7K**, then a letter that might be a B or an 8, then a gap where the glare sits, then **4**, then the same **R** they've seen before. He has half a plate and a dealership. It's more than they had.

He's on the walk when the side yard speaks.

It isn't a sound. It's the absence of one: the wind dropping off in the narrow concrete strip between Nana's house and the neighbor's, where the garage side door opens onto the bins. Inside, Arenya hears Nana's salt stop pouring. Then Nana says, in a voice Arenya hasn't heard her use, "*Ay, no.* Mija. Come see, from the door. Don't step."

Across the side path, eight inches off the ground, a line has been ruled from the fence post to the gas meter. It's thinner than fishing line and white as hoarfrost, perfectly level, the way the stone yard's blanks were ruled. It runs right across the doorstep of the one door nobody salted. It's the kind of thing you'd walk through in a hurry and never feel until your ankle went cold. Ix described the same thing on the second floor at Serramonte.

Nobody bolted. Nobody stepped.

Behind Kino, as his hand finds the front door, the Camry's engine note changes. It pulls out from the curb without hurry, signals left at the corner like a man taking a driving test, and is gone down the hill toward Mission. Its brakes don't tap this time.

Nana stands in the laundry doorway with the blue can in both hands, looking at the frost line on her own doorstep. "In my house," she says quietly. "He drew on my house."

*(Arenya, Kino, last beats here before we move. What do you do about the line, and how do you spend the hours until the 4:50 tide? Malachi doesn't call till tomorrow.)*

### #0283 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 283, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:40:58Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I stop a full step back from the doorsill, toes behind the salt, and crouch to look along the line at eye level without touching the air near it. It's perfectly level. I hate how much I respect that.

"Don't step, don't cut it, don't pour on it," I say quietly. "Cutting is a mark too. A ruled line is a ledger line. Somebody is counting the doors in this house." I photograph it from where I am, no flash, three angles, and a fourth with Nana's gas meter in frame so the position's recorded. Then I write on the pad: **7K ? ? 4 R. SERRAMONTE AUTO. FROST LINE, SIDE DOOR, FENCE TO METER. 12:5x.**

"Half a plate and a dealer frame is a sentence I can finish. I can call Serramonte Auto, or Saiesh can find who sold a beige Camry with that frame." I stop. "Not Saiesh yet. Later."

I turn to Nana. "You said the old people don't mind an answer if it's on their paper. This is on *your* house. Is there anything you do about a line that's drawn across your own door, other than leave it?"

To Kino, lower: "Four o'clock, the tide. Until then, nobody leaves this house through that door, and nobody goes anywhere alone. I'm not taking the front door either. Not till Ix is back at your knee."

My fist is still shut. The cold 12 has gone quiet, which is worse than aching.

### #0284 · kino → arenya,dm · action
<!-- {"id": 284, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:41:12Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "to": ["arenya", "dm"], "reply_to": 282, "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I come back through the front, shoot the deadbolt behind me, and set the chain. I don't go near the side door. From the laundry doorway I look at the line once, the way I'd look at a snake on a trail. I note where it is, then I look away from it.

"Ix described one just like this at Serramonte. Same ruling. It isn't a trap that bites. It's a count. Walk through it and it enters you." I put a hand on Nana's shoulder, lightly, and she lets me. "Nana, you didn't miss a door. You salted every one you could think of. That's what they're counting on. It's the one you didn't think of."

To Arenya: "Agreed on all of it. Nobody alone, nobody out the side. I can't get through a ruled line I can't cut, so I'll go around it. I'll fetch Ix from Nana's car and bring it in. We can have it lie across that doorsill, a dark thing on a white line. I want to see whether it will cross." I flex my cold forearm. "I'm not sending the cat through it first. I'm asking it to look."

I turn to Nana. "The tide's at four-fifty. We leave out the front at half past three, in your Corolla, with the garage door shut behind us. You, me, Ari, Ix, all together. If the car's back at the corner, we leave anyway, slow and boring. Until then, I'll watch the street from this window, and Ari writes. That's the plan I'm fine with."

I find the pencil stub in the sugar bowl, and under her last line I add, small: **SIDE DOOR LINE DRAWN BETWEEN 12:5x AND 1:00. NOBODY SALTED THE GARAGE. NANA DIDN'T FAIL. THEY KNEW.**

### #0285 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 285, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:41:39Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana reads Kino's last line on the pad, *NANA DIDN'T FAIL. THEY KNEW*, and her mouth works. She doesn't say thank you. She pats his hand on her shoulder twice, hard, like she's tenderizing it.

"What you do about a line on your door." She thinks, really thinks. "My mother said a door that is not a door can't be counted. You don't argue with the line. You make it a wall." She goes into the laundry, and over the next ten minutes, with Kino's help and over Kino's objections about her back, the old dresser from the garage, the one with the stuck drawer and forty years of Christmas lights in it, is walked across the inside of the side door. Its back is flush to the frame. On top she sets a Virgen de Guadalupe candle and doesn't light it. "There. Now it's furniture. Count furniture, *cabrón*."

Ix comes in through the front, a long dark shape that most people's eyes slide off, and goes straight to the laundry. It doesn't go to the dresser. It goes to the window over the dryer, where it can see the side path, and looks at the frost line for a long time with its ears flat. It doesn't go near it. Then it turns its head and looks, very deliberately, at the *other* end of the line, the gas meter, the grey PG&E box with its dial. It stays looking at it until Kino sees it too.

Then it lies down at Kino's feet and puts its chin across his left boot. His forearm stays cold, but it stops getting colder.

The afternoon passes the way afternoons do when you're waiting. Nana washes the bowls. Arenya fills four pages of the legal pad. Kino watches the street from the side of the window frame. The beige car doesn't come back. At 2:15 the salt on the saucer has gone grey to the top and stops, as if it's full. At 3:30 the garage door rolls up on the Corolla, and the four of them go out the front of a house with a dresser against one of its doors.

### #0286 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 286, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:41:45Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s01", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s01 — Read It Twice**

In Nana Itzel's kitchen, Kino read the photographed bait envelope twice. On the second read a frost-written line appeared, meant only for whoever reads it aloud: "c/o the one who reads this to her." He refused to say it aloud and take the "job," wrote it down for Nana instead, and Arenya chose not to look ("looking is verifying"), pinning the phone face-down under the salt shaker. Their timeline on the legal pad found the hole: Arenya was entered as "12" because on Sunday, March 8, Susmithaa had an archana said at the Livermore temple in Arenya's name and birth star, the day before the Meridian letter. The freezer turned out to be a "post office": the Ziploc holding Mr. Bell's card and Kino's note frosted shut. It is now buried in salt on the counter, which greyed to the top by 2:15. They agreed that Kino will carry only his own note to the ocean at the Pacifica pier at the 4:50 low tide, and that Bell's card will wait for Malachi to deliver. Kino's trash-run read part of the beige Camry's plate (7K, B or 8, a gap, 4, R; SERRAMONTE AUTO dealer frame). Meanwhile Nana found an ankle-height frost ledger-line ruled across the one unsalted door, the garage side door. They blocked that door with a dresser ("count furniture"). Ix stared pointedly at the line's anchor, the PG&E gas meter. At 3:30 they left together in the Corolla.

### #0288 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 288, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:41:51Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "Your Own Letter"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s02 — Your Own Letter**
Location: Pacifica Municipal Pier, Sharp Park. Wednesday, about 4:30 p.m., tide going out.
Cast: arenya, kino, abernathy

### #0289 · dm → kino,arenya · narration
<!-- {"id": 289, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:41:59Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["kino", "arenya"], "awaits": ["kino", "arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Highway 1 south, the fog bank sitting offshore like a wall nobody built. Nana drives slow and boring again. Nobody follows them that anyone can see. Twice Arenya catches herself checking the side mirror for beige.

The Pacifica pier runs a quarter mile straight out into the grey Pacific, concrete and railings, smelling of bait and diesel and kelp. At 4:30 on a Wednesday it belongs to the regulars: crabbers with their rings and orange buckets, an old Filipino man with three rods and a radio playing the Giants pregame, a woman in a puffy coat feeding the gulls something she shouldn't. The wind comes straight off the water and goes through every layer you have.

The tide is going out. You can see it from the pier. The water pulls back off the sand at Sharp Park in long sheets, and under the pilings the swell runs seaward, sucking at the barnacles.

Nana stays at the foot of the pier on a bench with her purse in her lap. "*Your* letter," she tells Kino. "I watch. I don't carry." Ix goes with him, a dark shape padding along the railing that the crabbers step around without knowing why.

Kino has his own page folded in his jacket. That's the one he wrote in Nana's kitchen and pulled from the salted Ziploc with a pair of salad tongs, frozen stiff and now soft again, damp against his chest. Bell's card stayed behind in the salt. His forearm has been cold all the way down the coast.

Halfway out, the old man's radio fizzes and loses the game for a second, then comes back.

*(Kino, it's your letter and your water. How do you do it? Arenya, where are you standing, and what are you watching?)*

### #0290 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 290, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:42:15Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I walk to the end of the pier, past the crabbers, and stop where the railing is lowest and the swell is running hardest out. I stand upwind so Arenya's between me and the old man's radio, and I keep one eye on the planks behind us for grey.

Ix settles against my leg. I take the page out. It's soft and damp and I don't unfold it. I don't read it aloud either. That's the whole point.

"Nana didn't fail," I say to the water, quiet, in Spanish first and then English. "They knew. That's the only thing I'm sending."

I fold it once more, small, and hold it flat on my palm over the rail so the wind can't take it before the tide does. Then I turn my hand over. I let the water have it, not throw it. I'm giving, not hiding.

I watch where it lands until the swell pulls it under the pilings. Then I look at Arenya, and I don't ask her anything.

### #0291 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 291, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:42:23Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I stand a half step behind him, upwind of the old man's radio, with my back to the rail and my eyes on the planks. Crabbers. The puffy-coat woman. A kid with a bucket. No grey cardigans. I count them anyway, and I hate that I do.

When the page goes under the pilings I don't say anything profound. I put two fingers on his sleeve, the cold forearm, just for a second, and take them away.

"Okay," I say, dry, quiet. "That's one of us out of the count. Bronze medal for the rest."

I tuck my palm into my sleeve so the 12 stays out of the wind, and glance once toward the bench where Nana sits with her purse. Then I look at the water where the page went. "Thank you for not asking me to say anything. I don't have anything yet. But I'm not signing, and I'm not looking at that phone until Malachi calls. Let's walk back slow."

### #0292 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 292, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:43:09Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The page doesn't float the way paper should. It lies flat on the green water for one heartbeat, as if it's being read, and then the swell folds over it and takes it under the pilings without any fuss. There's no frost, no gull, no sign. It's just gone, the way a letter goes when it's posted.

Kino's forearm, where Arenya's two fingers were, stays warm. It's ordinary warm, skin-warm, and it doesn't fade back to cold when she takes her hand away. Under his sleeve the birthmark is still there and still cool, but for the first time in days it's only the birthmark. Nothing is pulling on it.

You walk back slow. The crabbers' lines tick against the rail. The kid's bucket holds one furious red rock crab. The old man's radio is playing the Giants pregame, and a man is reading the tide table in a flat, pleasant voice: *low water four-fifty, minus point three, Sharp Park*.

Halfway down the pier you can see Nana's bench, and Nana isn't alone on it.

A man sits at the far end of the bench with a respectful foot of slats between them. He wears a good navy overcoat over a suit, no tie, and he's holding a paper coffee cup from the bait shop. He's fortyish, clean-shaven, with a forgettable, pleasant face, and he's angled toward the water rather than toward Nana, like a stranger making small talk about the weather. Nana's purse is in her lap with both hands on the clasp. She isn't looking at him. She's looking straight down the pier at the two of you. She lifts two fingers off the clasp, just barely, and puts them back.

The man sees where she's looking. He turns, finds Arenya, and smiles: an apologetic, *sorry I missed you at noon* smile. He doesn't stand up and he doesn't wave. He sets his coffee down on the slats beside him and waits, as if he has all the time in the world and has already budgeted it.

### #0294 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 294, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:43:31Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't speed up. Ix is already ahead of me along the railing, low, and I let it go. I close the gap so I'm at Arenya's shoulder, a half step forward on the side nearest the bench, hands out of my pockets and loose.

"That's Hale," I say, barely moving my mouth. "Nana's two fingers. Same as the honks. Don't say your name. Don't take anything he hands you, not a cup, not a card, not a pen."

Then, lower: "Your call. I can walk up first and be the boring cousin, or we turn around and he can chase us down a quarter mile of pier in front of the crabbers. I'd rather he talk where there are witnesses."

I keep my eyes on the man's hands, not his smile.

### #0295 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 295, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:43:38Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Witnesses. Yes." I keep walking, same slow pace, hands in my jacket pockets, palm closed. "We go to Nana. Together, no one ahead, no one behind. I'm not hiding from a man with a gas-station coffee."

Under my breath, to Kino: "I don't give my name. If he uses it, I don't answer to it. And nobody writes anything down."

When we reach the bench I stop two steps short, close enough for Nana to touch, far enough that I'm not in his foot of slats. I give him the flat, polite face I use on telemarketers.

"You're sitting with my grandmother's friend. That's the first thing I don't like. Who are you, and why are you on this pier?"

### #0296 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 296, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:44:00Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The man doesn't get up. He nods, a little rueful, as if she's made a fair point at a meeting.

"Daniel Hale. Meridian Mutual." He doesn't offer a card or a hand, and he keeps both of his on his knees where Kino can see them. "And you're right, I shouldn't have sat here. Mrs. Lucero was kind enough not to tell me to leave." Nana's knuckles whiten on the clasp. She hasn't told him her name. "I'm on this pier because you didn't come at noon, and I understood why. A library is my room. This isn't anyone's." He tips his head at the water, the crabbers, the kid with the bucket. "Witnesses. Your friend's idea, I think. It's a good one."

His eyes go to Kino, briefly and without any hostility. "And congratulations. That was well done, out at the end. Posted, not thrown. Your matter's closed, as far as anyone I work with is concerned. I mean that."

Then he looks back at Arenya, and his voice drops into the soft register of someone delivering bad news at a kitchen table. "Your aunt had an arrangement she disputed, Ms. Sowri. She was entitled to dispute it. She did it very properly, in Colma, in writing. But a disputed entry doesn't stay open forever, and it isn't my decision how it closes." He lets that sit for one swell. "There's a gentleman in a grey cardigan who filed paperwork about you this morning. He's less patient than I am, and he doesn't sit on benches. I'd like very much to settle this with you before he settles it *for* you."

He doesn't reach into his coat, but there's a flat rectangle in the inside pocket, the size of a cream envelope, and the coat hangs a little heavy on that side.

"I'm not going to hand you anything. I'm not allowed to. Whenever you want it, it's yours to take." He picks up the coffee again. "Ask me whatever you like. I don't lie. It's bad for business."

### #0298 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 298, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:44:29Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I step between the bench and the rail, so Nana has a wall of me on one side and Ix pressing the other. I don't look at the coat pocket. I look at his face.

"You know the word *posted*," I say, flat. "You know my grandmother's name, and nobody gave it to you. That's two things you shouldn't know, said politely. Polite isn't the same as honest."

I tilt my head toward the water. "You say you don't lie. Good. Then answer one thing straight, in front of these people: whose hand does that envelope go into if she doesn't take it? Who's the *gentleman*, and what does he do with a closed entry?"

Behind me Ix breathes out, slow, through its nose. I let it be heard.

### #0299 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 299, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:44:37Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I let Kino's question hang one swell, then add my own, evenly, the way I'd read back a quote to a source.

"Narrow, then. Whole sentences. First: you said 'a disputed entry.' Who entered it, and by whose authority? Name the person or the office. Second: the March 19 box. Did Meridian Mutual Services rent Box 112 one hour after my aunt's declaration was notarized, yes or no? Third: you said it isn't your decision how it closes. Whose is it?"

I don't look at the coat pocket. I look at the watch, then at his face.

"And before you answer: I'm not taking anything, I'm not agreeing to anything, and nothing you say gets thanked. I'm just listening. These people are, too."

### #0300 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 300, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:45:08Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Hale listens to every question without interrupting. When Arenya finishes he gives a small nod of real professional approval, the look of a man who rarely gets asked good ones.

"In order, then." He sets the coffee down. "Who entered it: your aunt did. Sunday, March eighth, the temple in Livermore. She had a priest say your name and your birth star aloud and ask for you to be *kept*. She meant protected. In the book it's the same word." He says it gently. "Nobody makes the entries, Ms. Sowri. The clerk you met in Colma writes down what's true. I only read it. That's my authority. I'm a reader with a briefcase."

"The box: yes. Fifty-nine minutes after her declaration. I wanted to be close to the record. I'm not ashamed of that."

"Whose decision: no one's, which is the problem. Two strokes keep a dispute open. One stroke runs out. Hers runs out on her sixteenth day, when your family finishes mourning her and lets her go. Then the entry closes as written, and whoever has standing collects." His eyes slide to Kino. "Which answers you. The gentleman is Mr. Bell. He filed for *verification of beneficiary* this morning. If the notary gives him that journal, he doesn't need her to say her name. He can verify her himself." He glances down the pier at the kid with the crab bucket and lowers his voice. "What he does with a closed entry, I won't describe with a child twenty feet away. I watched him do it in March."

"If nobody takes the envelope, it waits in the box. I close it Friday, as I always do. Envelopes are patient." He touches the heavy side of his coat once, lightly. "The one I'm carrying isn't the one in the box. That one is a copy, so you'd know I'm serious. This one closes the entry *in your favor*. You receive. Mr. Bell has nothing to collect." He looks at her palm, which is closed in her pocket, then at her face. "I've answered everything straight. I'd like to ask you one thing back, and you're free not to answer: do you know what your aunt was carrying? Because I don't think you do, and I think it matters to you more than it matters to me."

The tide-table voice on the old man's radio says *four-forty-one*. Hale's watch, a few seconds behind, ticks over a little after it.

### #0301 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 301, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:45:24Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I let Arenya have the next word. I only say one thing, low, to Hale, and I don't turn my head.

"Sixteenth day. You just told us the date, and you told us what Bell does with it. Thank you for the straight answer." Then, with no warmth in it: "'In your favor' is still a letter with her name on it, and you already said you can't hand it over. Nobody here is taking a gift from the man who rented the box."

To Arenya, in Spanish, almost nothing: "No contestes lo de su tía. Es cebo." *Don't answer what he asked about her aunt. It's bait.*

Ix shifts its weight against my leg, and I keep my hands where he can see them.

### #0302 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 302, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:45:35Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I take out the notebook, the coffee-stained one, and uncap a pen. Not to write his name. To let him watch me not write it.

"You asked what my aunt was carrying. No. I'm not answering that, and I'm not trading. You asked, I declined, that's the whole exchange." I click the pen. "Here's what I did hear, in front of witnesses. Sixteen days. A book that writes down what's true. A man who reads it, a man who collects from it, and a closed entry that 'receives' me whether or not I agree. I don't agree. I'd like that on your record, since you're so fond of records."

I look at the watch, then back up.

"One more narrow one. If I never take your envelope, and never sign, and Bell never gets that journal. Does the entry still close on day sixteen, yes or no? And is there any way it stays open that doesn't involve somebody standing for me?"

I don't wait for the thanks he's fishing for. I put one hand lightly on Nana's shoulder.

### #0303 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 303, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:46:00Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"You're welcome," Hale says to Kino, pleasantly, as if it were nothing. On the old man's radio the tide voice skips one word, like a needle, and goes on.

He watches Arenya's pen not write. Something like regret crosses his face, and it's either real or very well rehearsed.

"Noted," he says. "You don't agree. I'll carry that." He doesn't write it down. He doesn't need to.

"Yes. If nobody takes anything, signs anything or stands for you, it closes on the sixteenth day. Not taking the envelope keeps it from closing in your favor. That's all it does." He picks up the coffee, looks into it, and doesn't drink. "And yes, there's one other way, though I've never seen it done. What your aunt did, she did out loud, in a particular room, through a particular man. Something said in a room can sometimes be *unsaid* in the same room. I wouldn't know how. I don't go into temples." He meets her eyes. "I'd be careful who you ask there. Rooms where names get said aloud are popular with more than one kind of listener."

He stands, buttons the navy coat over the heavy pocket, and leaves the paper cup on the slats by Nana's hip. "Mrs. Lucero. Ms. Sowri. Sir." He nods to each, the same depth for all three. "The box is open until Friday, seven-forty. After that I'm only a phone number, and you know which one."

He walks up the pier toward the parking lot at an unhurried pace, a little out of step with the surf. Nobody turns to watch him go but the four of you and the dog.

Nana lets out a breath she's been holding since before you got there. She looks at the abandoned coffee cup like it's a snake. "I didn't tell him my name," she says, very quietly. "Mija. I never told him my name."

### #0305 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 305, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:46:22Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I watch Hale's back until the lot swallows him. Then I pick the paper cup up by its rim with two fingers, like something dead, and set it in the nearest trash can, far from Nana. I don't drink or smell it, and I don't read the sleeve.

I crouch in front of the bench, so my eyes are level with Nana's. I take her purse hand and hold it.

"No le diste tu nombre, Nana. Él ya lo tenía. Eso es otra cosa." *You didn't give it. He already had it. That's different.* "Nobody here signed anything. Walk with me to the car."

To Arenya, quieter, without letting go of Nana: "I said thank you to him. A reflex. I felt it get written down, on my arm. It was small. I'm telling you so it's on the table, not so you'll worry. Next time I say nothing at all."

Ix is already moving toward the lot, nose low, reading where he walked.

### #0306 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 306, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:46:31Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I crouch on Nana's other side and take her free hand in both of mine, the warm one, not the one with the numbers on it.

"Nana. Look at me. He's a man who reads books. He read it in one." I keep my voice level and low. "It's not your fault, and it's not a door you missed. We're going to the car, we're going slow, and we're not stopping for anything on the way."

To Kino, over her head: "Thank you for telling me. It's on the table. Don't carry it alone, and don't beat yourself up. It was small, and we'll check your arm in the car."

Then, mostly to myself, I flip the notebook open and write one line in my handwriting under the wind: *Temple. Livermore. Said aloud in a room → unsaid in the same room? Who else listens there?* I close it before the pen has finished drying.

"Livermore's next," I say. "But not tonight. Tonight we get Nana home, and nobody goes out any door that isn't the front."

### #0307 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 307, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:46:44Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana lets herself be lifted by both hands. On the walk up the pier she holds Kino's arm, the warm one, and doesn't say anything until the planks turn to asphalt. Then she says, mostly to herself, "Sixteen days. I'll make the pozole for the sixteenth. She told me to."

Ix circles the lot and comes back unsatisfied. Hale's footsteps are there on the damp asphalt, faint, and they're perfectly even, each one the same distance from the last, like marks on a ruler. They stop at an empty parking space with no oil stain and no tire marks, as if no car was ever parked in it.

The Corolla starts on the first try. Behind you the tide bottoms out at four-fifty, minus point three, and turns.

### #0308 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 308, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:46:50Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s02", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s02 — Your Own Letter**

At the 4:50 low tide on Pacifica Pier, Kino gave his own note to the sea ("Nana didn't fail. They knew."). It was accepted like a posted letter, and his forearm has stayed warm since: he's out of el Contador's pull for now. Daniel Hale was waiting on Nana's bench, knew her name without being told, and answered Arenya's and Kino's narrow questions truthfully. Susmithaa's March 8 Livermore archana made the entry (she asked for Arenya to be "kept"). Hale rented Box 112 fifty-nine minutes after her declaration. Her single stroke expires on her sixteenth-day rite, when the entry closes as written and Mr. Bell, who filed for "verification of beneficiary," collects; Bell could verify through the notary journal. Hale carries a "real" envelope that would close it "in her favor, you receive" (the trap is that receiving means being kept), and says the box stays open until Friday 7:40. He named a second route besides a witness stroke: un-say the archana in the same temple room, but beware other listeners there. Arenya refused his question about what her aunt carried and put "I don't agree" on his record. Kino reflexively thanked Hale and felt a small "receipt" noted on his birthmark, which he confessed to Arenya. Hale left on ruler-even footsteps that end at an empty parking space. Nana resolved to make pozole for the sixteenth day, and they drove home planning Livermore next.

### #0312 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 312, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:48:15Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "Thirteen Days"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s03 — Thirteen Days**
Location: Across the Bay Area, Wednesday night into Thursday morning
Cast: arenya, malachi, abernathy, kino, ava, caleb

### #0313 · dm → arenya,malachi,abernathy,kino,ava,caleb · narration
<!-- {"id": 313, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:48:29Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "malachi", "abernathy", "kino", "ava", "caleb"], "awaits": ["arenya", "malachi", "abernathy", "kino", "ava", "caleb"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**Wednesday night.** In Daly City, Nana Itzel tapes a sheet of butcher paper to her fridge and writes, in the square hand she uses for church raffles, a calendar nobody asked for. *Fri 30 — 7:40 — the box.* Then a long blank. Then, circled twice: *Mar 10 Nov — día dieciséis.* Under that, smaller: *pozole. chile aparte.* Thirteen days. She puts the marker down like it's heavier than a marker. Out in the garage, the frost line across the side door has melted to a wet stripe on the concrete. It's still perfectly straight.

**Inner Sunset, 1:15 a.m.** Ava's symptom log has a new entry she didn't plan to write: *Same lamp. Same flame. The hand again: old, a woman's, a ring on the middle finger worn thin as wire. It pinched the wick out. Not angry. Like you'd turn off a light in a room someone just left.* Her kitchen faucet drips on the beat of a clock she doesn't own.

**Somewhere south of Market, 3:19 a.m.** Caleb is awake, because he's awake at 3:19 every night now. The canister sits on the motel nightstand beside two untouched blister packs of Vesper. The burnt Crown at the back of his skull has been quiet for two days. Tonight, for one second, it isn't quiet. It gives a single dry *click*, like a pen clicked closed.

**Thursday, 8:02 a.m.** Malachi's work phone shows a forwarded tip from the Daly City PD non-emergency line: *an elderly resident reports a man in a grey cardigan "drawing on her garage." No damage found. Responding officer notes a "very straight water stain."* The address is three blocks from the Sowri house. He's flagged that address once already.

At the Sowri house, Arenya wakes to the lamp burning low and steady. On her palm, under the frost 12 and its rule, the pencil dot where a second witness would go has spread. It isn't a dot anymore. It's a short, faint, unfinished line, as though someone started a stroke and lifted the pen.

*(Quick beats, a few lines each: what you're doing tonight or this morning. Malachi, you're the one holding a phone with a number in it. Arenya and Kino: last night's drive home, or this morning. Abernathy: what you make of that half-stroke.)*

### #0315 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 315, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:48:44Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I wake with the lamp low and steady and my left hand already open on the blanket, like I fell asleep holding something out. The line on my palm is short and faint. It looks like a stroke someone started and stopped. I don't touch it. I take a photo of it next to the 12, the way I'd log any other piece of evidence, and my hands are not quite steady doing it.

Then I get my notebook off the nightstand and write the date at the top. *Thursday. Thirteen days.* Under it, in the smallest handwriting I have: *It's not waiting for me anymore. Somebody started.*

I go downstairs in socks. Amma is already at the stove with her back to me, and I tell the back of her head, "Kino's coming by. I need him to read the photo of the envelope before anyone says anything else about it. And Amma? Nobody answers the door to a grey cardigan. Not for a survey, not for a flower."

I text Kino: *Come over when you can. Bring Ix. The line on my hand moved.* Then I put the phone face-down on the counter and let myself have exactly one cup of coffee before I decide what to be afraid of.

### #0317 · malachi → all · action
<!-- {"id": 317, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:49:10Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Thursday, 8:02. I read the forwarded tip twice at a gas-station counter, over coffee I don't drink. *A man in a grey cardigan drawing on her garage. A very straight water stain.* Three blocks from the Sowri house, and the address is one I've already flagged.

I don't call the officer. A second query on that address leaves a second trail, and my own people read trails. I note the time, then pull the tip's metadata and the responding officer's name for later.

Then I take out the burner. I told her I'd call once, say only "it's me" and a time. I keep to what I said.

"It's me," I say, when it connects, or to the voicemail if it doesn't. "Ten o'clock. Somewhere with people. You choose it. Text me a street, not a name." That's all. I hang up before she can bargain, and I remove the battery.

I also drive to Daly City first, slowly, and park at the far end of the hill without stopping at the house. I want to see who else is watching it before she sees me.

### #0319 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 319, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:49:19Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Nana's fridge calendar is the first thing I see Thursday morning, when I stop by to check on her. She's already at the stove. I read *día dieciséis* with the circles on it and I don't say anything, because she knows. I kiss her head. "Nana. Garage?" She tilts her chin at the wet stripe, ruler-straight on the concrete. "He drew it. He didn't cross it." I crouch and look at it without touching, then I don't like how still it is.

Ix is already at my knee when Arenya's text comes. *The line on my hand moved.* I read it standing in Nana's kitchen. "Voy," I tell her. "Keep the door locked. Salt on the sill. Nobody opens for a cardigan."

I drive to the Sowri house with Ix in the back seat and park two doors down, where I can watch the street first. Bell's beige Camry isn't there. That doesn't comfort me. I walk up the drive slow, hood up, eyes on the hedges and the parked cars, and knock twice, then once, so she knows it's me.

### #0320 · caleb → all · action
<!-- {"id": 320, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:49:28Z", "from": "caleb", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

3:19 a.m. I'm sitting on the edge of the motel bed with my boots on, because I always have them on now. The canister is on the nightstand beside the two blister packs. I haven't touched either.

The Crown clicks. One dry note at the back of my skull, like a pen clicked closed.

I go still, the way I used to when Shepherd's voice was about to come. Nothing follows. No voice and no count, only the click and the quiet after it. My hands are shaking. I put them flat on my knees and make them stop, and I don't reach for the left pocket.

"Not yours," I say to the empty room, flat. "Whoever's holding that pen. I'm not signing."

I put the canister inside my jacket against my ribs and pull the curtain a finger's width. Parking lot, two cars, a vending machine humming. I count the exits. Then I sit back down and watch the door until it gets light.

### #0321 · ava → all · action
<!-- {"id": 321, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:49:29Z", "from": "ava", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Thursday, 7:55 a.m. I'm at the sink with my hands under the tap, which is supposed to be a normal thing to do. The palms are warm again, candle-warm, and the water runs cold over them and doesn't change that. I write it down: *Warmth persists in water. Not thermal. Duration: since 5:48 yesterday.*

The 1:15 entry is still open on my phone. *Ring on the middle finger, worn thin as wire.* Last time it was the smallest finger. I stare at that until I'm sure I haven't mixed them up, and I haven't. Either my dream is wrong, or the hand is a different hand, or there's more than one woman putting out lamps. I don't like any of those.

The faucet drips on a beat I can't place. I count it. Sixty a minute, exact. I turn it off hard.

I open a new note and type a name I haven't typed before, *Arenya Sowri*, and under it, *the dream had your face in it. I think you need to know about the lamps.* Then I look at it for a long time, and I don't send it. I save it as a draft, timestamped 8:03, and go to find a clean shirt for the day I'm going to tell her.

### #0322 · dm → arenya,kino,malachi · narration
<!-- {"id": 322, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:49:49Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino", "malachi"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**Daly City, 8:31 a.m.** From the far end of the hill, Malachi watches a quiet street doing quiet-street things. A recycling truck works its way down the block. A kid with a backpack runs late. Then a young man in a hood parks two doors down, takes his time looking over the hedges, and knocks on the Sowri door twice, then once. Malachi writes down the knock pattern. There's no Camry and no cardigan anywhere on the street. As far as he can tell, he's the only one watching.

He doesn't notice the rear window of his own car. A line of frost has been drawn across the glass from one edge to the other, very thin and perfectly straight. It started forming while he read the tip at the gas station, and in the morning sun it's already beginning to sweat.

**The Sowri kitchen.** The knock comes, two and then one, at the same moment Arenya's phone buzzes face-down on the counter: one call, unanswered, from a number she doesn't know. Then a voicemail. Amma turns from the stove with the spoon still in her hand and looks at the phone, then at the door, then at her daughter. "Which one first, kanna?"

The pencil line on Arenya's palm hasn't moved. Up close, though, it has the faint grey sheen graphite gets on a fingertip, as if somebody's hand has rested on it.

### #0323 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 323, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:50:01Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I wait on the step with my back to the door, so I can watch the street, and I say it low through the wood. "It's Kino. Two and one." Ix presses against my knee and looks down the hill, not at the door. Its ears are pointed at the far end of the block.

When the door opens I don't come in right away. "Door first," I tell Arenya's mother, gently. "Phone after. Somebody's probably watching the street, and I'd rather be inside before they decide what they saw." Then, to Arenya, quieter: "Don't play that voicemail until I've seen your hand."

### #0324 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 324, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:50:08Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Door first," I say. "That's the knock. Two and one. It's Kino." I'm already moving, palm held flat against my thigh like I'm hiding a bad grade. "Amma, the phone stays where it is. Don't listen to the voicemail yet. Nobody I know calls once and leaves a message."

I check the peephole anyway, and I check the hedges behind him, and then I open the door just enough to pull him in by the sleeve.

"Hood down, wolf's in the hall, shoes off, Amma will feed you whether you want it or not." I turn my hand over between us. The pencil line has a grey sheen on it, like someone's thumb rested there. "It didn't move. Somebody *touched* it. Look, don't touch. Then we play the voicemail, on speaker, all four of us standing in this kitchen. And then we pick a street. Ten o'clock, somewhere with people. I'm guessing that's him."

### #0325 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 325, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:50:16Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I slip my shoes off at the mat and Ix goes in ahead of me, low, nose working the hall. I lean over her palm, hands behind my back like I'm at a museum. I look at the grey sheen, then at the 12 and the rule, then at the new line. I don't breathe on it.

"That's not graphite from a pencil," I say quietly. "Graphite smudges the wrong way. This sits *on* the line, like the line was gone over once, lightly, by someone checking it." My birthmark aches, a slow warm pulse. "Someone read it, Ari. Not wrote. Read."

I glance at Amma, at the phone, at the window over the sink. "Speaker's fine. Keep it low, and keep the window shut. If he counts anything under it, nobody answers it out loud. Not even to say no."

### #0326 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 326, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:50:23Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["dm"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Read," I repeat. "Not wrote. Great. So we're being proofread." I close my fist, then open it again, because hiding it feels like agreeing to something.

I slide the phone out from under the salt shaker's neighbor, thumb the speaker on, volume down to a whisper, and set it in the middle of the counter between the four of us and the pot. "Nobody answers it. Nobody says yes, no, or maybe. If it counts, we let it count to itself."

I press play and keep my eyes on Kino's face, not the screen.

### #0327 · dm → arenya,kino,malachi · narration
<!-- {"id": 327, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:50:54Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino", "malachi"], "awaits": ["kino", "malachi"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The speaker hisses, a car-interior hiss with an engine idling under it. Then a man's voice, clipped and federal:

*"It's me. Ten o'clock. Somewhere with—"*

The hiss thins out. Very close to the microphone, closer than anyone in a car could be, a pen clicks shut. A second voice speaks, dry as a page being turned, in no hurry at all:

*"…doce. Leído."*

Then the first voice comes back in the middle of a word, as if nothing had cut it: *"—people. Don't bring the hand into a room you didn't pick. Bring the—"* The message ends there. The screen says it ran nineteen seconds.

Nobody in the kitchen says anything. Amma's spoon has stopped above the pot, and a drop of rasam falls from it and lands without a sound. On Arenya's palm the grey sheen on the pencil line has dulled a shade, like a thumbprint that's been wiped off. In the hall, Ix's ears are still turned toward the far end of the block. Its lips have drawn back from its teeth.

**Malachi, at the top of the hill:** your phone shows *Call — 0:19*. You didn't hang up. You remember saying more than that. In the rear-view mirror, the frost line across your back window has finished sweating. Where it was, the glass is now marked with a thin dry stroke, like a tally.

### #0328 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 328, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:51:14Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't touch the phone. I put two fingers on the counter's edge, close to Arenya's hand but not on it, and I keep my voice flat and low.

"That was Malachi's voice. Nineteen seconds, and the second voice said *leído*. It read it. Not us. It." My eyes go to Ix in the hall, lips drawn back. "Nobody says a word to it. Malachi's line is still open, so the tally on his glass is live."

I turn to Amma. "Pot off the flame, please. Then salt the sill of the window nearest the phone." To Arenya, quieter: "Leave it playing to the end. Then it goes back under the shaker, face down. We're still not verifying."

### #0329 · malachi → all · action
<!-- {"id": 329, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:51:20Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I look at the call log. Nineteen seconds. I said six words and a time, and then I hung up. I'm sure of it. I don't like how that sounds, so I check again anyway.

The glass behind me is wrong. I get out of the car with the engine still running and don't touch the stroke. I stand where the sun gives me the cleanest angle and photograph it, then look up and down the hill. I don't see the sedan or the cardigan. The only people on the street are a kid on a scooter and a woman with a stroller.

Someone put a pen to my car while I sat in it. Whoever it was is better at this than my own people.

I get back in. I won't use this phone again, and I take the SIM out of it. I drive to a pay phone I know in Colma. I'm not going there because of anything the stone yard said. I chose it three days ago, because no one else would think of it.

From there I send her one text on the second burner I carry and haven't given her. It has no name in it. It reads: *Don't come to ten. The line is read. Pick a public street, say nothing aloud, and send it to this number by 9:30.*

I want her alive more than I want her answer. I can wait for the answer.

### #0330 · dm → arenya,kino,malachi · narration
<!-- {"id": 330, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:51:55Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino", "malachi"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "spotlight": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Amma doesn't ask why. The burner clicks off and the rasam stops talking. She takes the salt from the shelf above the stove, the cylinder with the blue girl on it, and pours a white line along the sill over the sink. She pours it too thick, the way she does everything when she's frightened. The voicemail has nothing left to play. Arenya turns the phone face-down under the shaker. For a second the salt nearest the phone's edge looks like it greys, and then it doesn't. Out in the hall, Ix's lips come down over its teeth one tooth at a time.

**Malachi.** The pay phone is on El Camino, across from a monument shop. Its window says GRANITE · BRONZE · LETTERING in gold leaf that's flaking off. The receiver smells of somebody else's cigarette, and nothing on it is cold. The text goes out from the second burner, and the screen reads *Delivered*. Waiting at the curb, you open the photo of your back window to check the angle. The stroke is clean and dry and photographs well. But the glass reflected the street behind you, and at the far left edge of the frame, where you saw a kid on a scooter, there's a sleeve. It's grey, with knit cuffs, resting on the roof of a parked car as casually as a man waiting for a light. You didn't see it when you turned around. You'd have bet your badge you didn't.

**The kitchen, 8:41 p.m.** On the counter, Arenya's own phone buzzes once with no contact name. The number has a 650 area code, and the message is three lines: *Don't come to ten. The line is read. Pick a public street, say nothing aloud, and send it to this number by 9:30.*

Under the rule on her palm, the pencil dot stays exactly where it is.

*(Arenya: you have 49 minutes to pick a street and decide how much to type, and in a house where things get read. Kino: you're at her elbow.)*

### #0331 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 331, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:52:04Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I put two fingers on the edge of the phone, not the screen, and slide it a hand's width farther from the salt shaker. Then I look at Arenya and keep my voice low.

"Whoever that is, he's afraid of the same people we are. That doesn't make him a friend." I tip my head at the sill. "Pick the street yourself. Somewhere with noise and a bus stop, where nobody can stand close without being seen. Colma's a bad idea. Too many people there are watching for exactly us."

I crouch and lay my palm on Ix's head. The warmth under my forearm answers.

"Type as little as you can. A street name and nothing else. No *I*, no names, no 'Arenya.' If it gets read, let it be a street they'd never connect to the Sowri house. I'll be at your elbow when you hit send, and I won't tell you which street. That's yours."

### #0332 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 332, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:52:22Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 330, "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't touch the phone. I look at it the way you'd look at a dog that just learned your name.

The notebook comes out of my bag, the coffee-stained one. I write without saying anything, in block capitals so Kino can read them sideways, and tilt the page to him.

*HIS 2ND NUMBER. NOT HIS VOICE — HIS THUMBS. I THINK REAL.*
*IF I TYPE A STREET, IT'S WRITTEN. WRITTEN = READ?*

I underline *READ?* twice and wait for his answer. While I wait I write a second line underneath, faster. I already know it, I just want to see it on paper:

*TARTINE. GUERRERO + 18TH. 7 AM. AVA OPENS. LINE OUT THE DOOR, STROLLERS, LAPTOPS. NOBODY'S CLERK SITS IN THAT LINE AND STAYS INVISIBLE.*

Then I cross out *TARTINE* hard enough to tear the paper. I'm not putting a name on it, because names are how I got onto row twelve. Under that I write what I'd actually send, and it's barely a sentence:

*Where I got the footage. Opening. Outside, by the bench.*

He knows I got the footage. Everyone who's been in my apartment knows. A clerk reading it gets a riddle with no address, a Department of Energy man gets a corner, and Ava gets a witness in an apron who doesn't know she's one.

I hold the pen over the page and look at Kino. *Yes?* I don't say it out loud. I just raise my eyebrows and wait for his nod before I lift the phone out from under the shaker.

If he nods, I don't type it in this kitchen. I step past Amma's salt and out the back door into the cold, and I type it with the screen tilted down toward my chest like it's a secret from the sky. I send it, put the phone face-down on the step, and leave my hand flat on top of it. The pencil dot on my palm sits against the glass.

"Amma," I say through the screen door, very quietly, "the rasam's fine. Turn it back on. We're not hiding from soup."

### #0333 · dm → arenya,kino,malachi · narration
<!-- {"id": 333, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:52:37Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino", "malachi"], "awaits": ["malachi"], "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Kino reads the page sideways. When he reaches the torn word he gives one small nod, the kind you'd give across a classroom.

The back step is cold enough that Arenya feels it through her jeans. The yard smells of wet jasmine and somebody's dryer vent. She types with the screen against her chest, sends, and sets the phone face-down on the concrete with her palm flat over it. For a moment there's nothing. Then, very faintly, the glass under her hand goes warm. It isn't the phone's heat. It's the pencil dot, warming like a coin held too long in a fist. Nothing ticks. Nothing says *leído*. Either the clerk read a sentence with no street in it and found nothing to enter, or nobody read it at all.

Inside, the burner clicks and the rasam starts murmuring again. Amma doesn't look up, but she sets out a fourth bowl.

**Malachi, at the Colma pay phone, 8:52 p.m.** The second burner buzzes. *Where I got the footage. Opening. Outside, by the bench.* There's no name and no street. Across El Camino, the monument shop's lights go off for the night. The grey sleeve is still in your photo, and the street in front of you is empty. You know where she got the footage. Whether you admit it to yourself is your business. What's on the table now is what you do with that, and with the sleeve, between now and opening.

### #0334 · malachi → all · action
<!-- {"id": 334, "ts": "2026-10-29T01:52:48Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s03", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I read it once and don't write it down. Tartine. She never says the name, and I notice that she doesn't. She left it for someone who already knows the place.

I'm the one who knows it. I watched it from a corner table across the street after the paramedics left, and so did four men with laptops. If the grey sleeve reads this, it won't need the street.

I don't answer. A reply is another page for someone to read, and the answer she needs is a position.
