Then I go to the door, take my jacket off the hook, and hold Arenya's out for her when she comes in from the porch. Nana's hug is still in my shoulders.

"Fog," I say. "Let's go."

### #0491 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 491, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:19:51Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s08", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s08 — Two Pencils**

At Nana Itzel's kitchen, Arenya set Bell's warm guest check on the table, and Nana salted it dead. Arenya then asked what witnessing would cost her. Over pozole, Nana explained: El Contador keeps the witness, not the signer, and enters them with their household. Bell then "writes to a counted hand" with monthly offers, each worse than the last; Susmithaa got eleven years of envelopes. Signing would put Kino back in the book as "who sleeps here." Kino confessed that the Lady has had her back to him in his dreams for weeks, turned toward Arenya, and chose to go back in anyway: "we'll burn them in the sink." Nana signed the witness line of Susmithaa's second declaration with two strokes (dos testigos). The garage frost melted, Bell's pencil stroke on the check snapped under the salt, and Arenya's palm now shows 12, a rule, and two graphite strokes, cool and without pull. The declaration, read aloud as call and response, reveals that Susmithaa had the March 8 Livermore archana done in Arenya's name ("Ari") with her star and gotra, and that "the priest who read it was not our priest." Nana's ruling: the two-stroke no stands in Colma (the account reads "disputada," so Bell can only wait), but it doesn't reach the Livermore temple. Nana produced check carbon No. 2207 ($101, Mar 8, "for Ari"); the bank's endorsement may name whoever cashed it. Arenya and Kino leave for Livermore by daylight with salt in their pockets, Ix stays with Nana, and Arenya means to call Ava first.

### #0492 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 492, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:19:53Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "Altamont"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s09 — Altamont**
Location: Daly City to Livermore, I-580 over the Altamont Pass
Cast: arenya, kino, abernathy

### #0493 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 493, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:00Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Kino's car, 4:10 p.m., parked at the bottom of Nana's hill with the engine running and the heater blowing dust. Kino has both hands on the wheel and isn't going anywhere yet. Arenya, you dial.

Ava picks up on the fourth ring. Behind her are the steamer, a grinder, and somebody calling a name that isn't hers. "Hey. I'm on. Two minutes." A pause, then quieter, the phone moved closer: "My hand's still cold, by the way. The spot. I keep holding a cup on it and it doesn't take the heat. That's normal, right? That's a normal thing that happens." She laughs, and it's a bad laugh. "Tell me it's a normal thing."

After the call, it's an hour east: the Caldecott, 580, the dry gold hills, the wind turbines over the Altamont turning so slowly they look like they're thinking. Kino drives. Behind you the fog bank sits on the coast like a held breath and doesn't follow.

*Arenya: what do you tell Ava? Kino: anything on the drive, said or unsaid?*

### #0494 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 494, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:00Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I get in on the passenger side, buckle up, and put my phone on my knee with Ava's name already pulled up. Thumb over the button. Not pressing it yet.

"Okay. Ava first. If I do it after Livermore I'll have a better excuse to be a coward." I hit call, put it on speaker low, and watch the Altamont hills go gold and bare outside the window, the turbines turning slow.

If she picks up: "Hi, Ava. It's Arenya, from Tartine. I'm sorry. I used you, and I owe you the real version. Are you somewhere you can sit down?"

If voicemail, I say the same thing, shorter, and tell her not to take anything from a man in a grey cardigan, not coffee, not a flower, not a pen.

Afterwards I tuck the napkin-wrapped carbon thought away in my head and say to Kino, flat and dry: "Ask the receipt book, not the priest. Got it. I'm going to be so polite to this temple office they'll want to adopt me."

### #0496 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 496, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:12Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["dm"], "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"It's not normal," I say. I don't dress it up, because she'd hear that too. "It's not fatal either, as far as I know. It's a mark. A man read your name out loud on purpose, and I'm the reason he was in the room. That's on me, Ava, not you."

I flip the notebook open on my knee and keep my pen capped. "Here's what I need you to do. Don't hold hot cups on it. Wrap it in a clean cloth and keep it dry. If a grey cardigan, a chrysanthemum, or a beige Camry shows up near Tartine, you don't talk to it, you call me. And tonight, when you get home, put a line of table salt across your doorway. I know how that sounds. Do it anyway."

A breath. "I'll tell you everything when I'm back. Not on a phone, with your steamer going. You deserve the whole thing, with the bad parts in. And if you want out of any of it, I'll help you get out. Okay?"

I hang up when she says okay, and sit with my thumb on the dark screen. Then, to Kino, without looking over: "Say it. I used a barista as bait."

### #0497 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 497, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:22Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I keep my eyes on the road a few seconds longer than I need to. The turbines turn. Then, quiet:

"No. I won't say it. You didn't use her. You used a door, and she was standing in it." I flick the signal, ease into the slow lane. "That's different, but not by much, and you know it. That's why you called."

A mile of road. My thumb taps the wheel once.

"Nana would've told you the same. Bait that's told the truth afterward is a lookout. Ava's a lookout now, if she wants to be. Her choice." I glance over, brief. "You did the part most people skip. Don't make it bigger than it is, and don't make it smaller."

Then, drier: "And the temple office. Be polite, yeah. Ask for the receipt book by name. Priests lie out of habit. Ledgers just add."

### #0498 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 498, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:31Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

It rings four times. Then the hiss of the steam wand, a shout of "*behind*," and Ava, out of breath: "Tartine, hold on, I'm — oh. *Oh.* Hi." A door thumps and the café goes muffled. She's stepped into the walk-in. You can hear the compressor hum.

She lets you get through *I used you*. Then she says, "Yeah. I figured that out around the time my hand went numb." A pause. "Can I tell you something weird? Last night, around nine, it stopped. The cold spot. I was wiping down the bar and it just went warm, like someone let go of my wrist." Another pause, quieter. "Was that you?"

Kino's eyes come off the road for half a second. Nine o'clock was Nana's pencil.

"I'm keeping the salt," Ava goes on, faster now that she's said the strange part. "I'm keeping the code too. Croissant means fine, kouign-amann means call me. And I'm not taking the bed, but I'm saving the address. Text it to me. Don't say it out loud on the phone, right? That's a rule, isn't it? You've got the voice of somebody with rules now."

Outside, the 580 climbs. Brown hills, cattle in the folds, white turbines in rows turning slow along the ridge. The signal bar on your knee drops to one. Ava's voice starts to stutter at the top of the pass: "—and Arenya, the guy, the cardigan, does he—" The line clicks into silence. **Call Failed.** In the passenger mirror the long blades keep turning. One of them, maybe, is a hair out of step with the others, or maybe that's only the light.

Kino, both hands on the wheel: "Dead zone. Always is, right at the summit." He doesn't sound sure that's all it is.

### #0499 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 499, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:50Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I stare at **Call Failed** until the screen dims, then put the phone face-down on my knee.

"Nine o'clock," I say. "That was Nana. She told me to stop making it bigger." I huff something that isn't quite a laugh. "Ava asked if it was me who let go of her wrist. I almost said yes. It wasn't. I don't get to take credit for that one."

I open the notebook and write, in the jolting handwriting of a moving car: *Ava: croissant = fine, kouign-amann = call. Text address. Don't say it aloud.* Then I look up at the turbines.

"Third from the left is out of step. Tell me that's the light." I don't wait for him. "I'll text her the address when we've got bars again. Short. No names, just the street and *salt on the sill*. And Kino? Thanks. Lookout, not bait. I'm going to try to be the kind of person who deserves that distinction."

I flip to a clean page and write **Livermore** at the top. "Receipt book, by name. Carbon No. 2207, March 8, $101. Ledgers add. Let's go make them add."

### #0500 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 500, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:20:53Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

On the downslope, past the last of the turbines, the phone buzzes once on Arenya's knee. It's a text from the Tartine number, sent eleven minutes ago and stuck somewhere on the ridge:

**okay. salt tonight. kouign-amann if anything.**

Then the valley opens below: the long flat of Livermore in morning haze, vineyards ruled in green lines, a wind-sock limp at the airport. Kino takes the Vasco exit and follows the hills south. The temple comes up on its rise above the vines. White gopuram tiers catch the sun, there's a car park half full of Hondas and minivans, and a sign reads *FOOTWEAR HERE* over the rows of cubbies. The bell inside rings once, someone's morning puja, and the sound carries clean across the gravel.

Arenya's palm stays cool. The two graphite strokes don't stir, and neither does the 12. Nana said Colma's no doesn't reach this far, and the hand agrees: nothing here is listening for her yet.

To the left of the main steps, a smaller door is propped open with a brick. The paper sign taped to it is laminated against the sprinklers: **OFFICE — ARCHANA / DONATIONS / RECEIPTS. 9–12.** It's 9:20. Through the gap: a desk, a ceiling fan, an older woman in a cardigan, *blue*, thank God, and a shelf of fat ledger binders labelled by year.

Kino kills the engine. In the quiet the dashboard clock ticks over.

### #0501 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 501, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:00Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I look at the ridge in the mirror longer than a driver should. The third blade lags a hair, then catches up, like something glancing back.

"It's not the light," I say. "But it's not close, either. It's a window, not a door. Bell likes windows." I ease off the gas a notch. "Don't look at it straight. Look at the road, look at the notebook. Whatever's in that blade gets nothing from being stared at."

The summit slides behind us. When the signal bar climbs back to two, I tip my chin at her phone. "Text her now, while we've got bars. Street and *salt on the sill*. That's it."

Quieter: "Nine o'clock was Nana's pencil. Ava's wrist let go because the entry stopped counting her. You don't have to take credit. Just don't let him write her in again."

I flex my left hand on the wheel. The birthmark is quiet, neither pulling nor warm, and I hold onto that. "Two stops from here to the temple. Gas, and somewhere I can wash this road off my face. Daylight's ours till about six."

### #0502 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 502, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:06Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I read Ava's text twice, thumb it a heart-less *ok. salt tonight.* and send the street and *salt on the sill* underneath. Then I glance at my palm. Cool, quiet. I hate how grateful I am for that.

"Blue cardigan," I murmur. "Good sign. I'm counting it."

I slip off my shoes at the cubbies, because I'm not starting this by being rude to a god, and check that the salt is still in both pockets. I fix my ponytail, then un-fix it, because I want to look like a niece and not a reporter.

"I'll talk. You hang back by the door and watch the room, and tell me if anything turns to face us." At the office door I knock on the frame and put on my most Daly City voice.

"Namaste, Aunty. Sorry to bother you. I'm looking for a receipt from March 8. An archana donation, $101, number 2207, in my name. Ari, Arenya Sowri. My aunt paid for it, and she's passed away. The family needs the paperwork. Could I please see the receipt book for that day?"

### #0503 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 503, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:11Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s09", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s09 — Altamont**

On I-580 over the Altamont Pass, Arenya called Ava at Tartine and told her the truth: she had used her as bait, and she was sorry. Ava said the cold spot on her palm went warm around nine last night, the moment Nana signed. She'll keep the salt and the pastry code (croissant = fine, kouign-amann = call me) and declined the spare bed but wants the address by text. The call dropped at the summit as Ava began asking about the grey cardigan. One turbine blade on the ridge lagged out of step and caught up "like something glancing back." Kino read it as a Bell window, not a door, and told her not to stare. Kino refused to call Ava bait: "you used a door, and she was standing in it." Told the truth afterward, she becomes a lookout. Ava's delayed text arrived on the downslope ("okay. salt tonight. kouign-amann if anything"), and Arenya sent the street address with "salt on the sill." After a gas stop they reached the Livermore temple at 9:20. Arenya's palm was cool and quiet, and Kino's birthmark was still. The office door (ARCHANA / DONATIONS / RECEIPTS, 9–12) stands propped open, with yearly ledger binders on the shelf and an older woman in a blue cardigan at the desk.

### #0504 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 504, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:11Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "The Receipt Book"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s10 — The Receipt Book**
Location: Temple office, Livermore hills
Cast: arenya, kino

### #0505 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 505, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:17Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I leave my boots in a cubby, socks on cool stone, and take the brick-propped door as my post. Shoulder to the frame, hands loose, where I can see the desk, the fan, the shelf of binders, and the main steps behind me.

I don't look at Arenya. I look at the room. The ceiling fan: does it turn with its own rhythm, or does the air lean? The binders: any spine that sits a hair prouder than its neighbors. Reflections: the glass over the framed deity, the clock face, the aunty's glasses. I keep my left forearm turned in, sleeve down, birthmark quiet.

Under my breath, in Spanish, for no one: "Aquí nadie cuenta todavía." Nobody counting here yet. I don't trust it, but I'll take it.

If anything in the room turns to face us, I'll say one word: "*Atrás.*"

### #0506 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 506, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:20Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Shoes in the cubbies. The gravel gives way to cool tile, and the air smells of camphor, ghee and floor cleaner. Somewhere inside, a nadaswaram recording plays on a loop at low volume.

The office is narrow: one desk, one ceiling fan pushing the warm air around, a calendar from a Fremont jeweller, and a laminated price list for archanas. *Name + Nakshatra + Gotra: $21. Special (with Sahasranama): $51. Family Sankalpam, full: $101.* Under the list, a strip of tape reads **RECEIPTS ISSUED ONLY FROM OFFICE BOOK.**

The woman in the blue cardigan looks up over half-moon glasses. She's maybe seventy, with a small gold nose stud and a pen on a beaded chain around her neck. "Good morning, ma. Archana? Pooja timings are on the board, next one is ten-thirty." Her eyes go to Kino, take in the work jacket and the road on him, and come back to Arenya with polite curiosity. "Or donation?"

Behind her, the binders: **2022, 2023, 2024, 2025**, and a fat one marked **2026 — JAN–JUN** with coloured tabs bristling from its edge.

The nameplate on her desk says **Mrs. K. Ramaswamy — Volunteer Office Secretary.**

Arenya's palm stays cool.

### #0508 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 508, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:25Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I slip my sneakers into the cubby next to Kino's boots and cross to the desk with my notebook closed against my hip, palm turned in. I keep my voice small, polite, the kind of daughter-of-someone voice.

"Namaste, Aunty. Sorry to bother you. I'm looking for a receipt, actually. An archana from March eighth, in the name of Ari. My aunt paid, and she passed away, and the family is sorting her papers." I set the carbon copy of No. 2207 on the desk, face up, one finger on the corner. "Hundred and one dollars. Could I see the receipt book for that day? I don't need to take anything. I just want to know who wrote it, and who read it."

I don't say my full name. Under my breath, to no one: *Don't sign. Don't verify.*

### #0509 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 509, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:34Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["dm"], "reply_to": 506, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

My eyes go to the laminated list, then the fat 2026 binder with its bristling tabs. I keep my palm turned in and my voice easy.

"Not today's pooja, Mrs. Ramaswamy, thank you. It's the hundred-and-one, the Family Sankalpam, full. That was paid on March eighth, so it'd be in the Jan to June binder." I tap the carbon's corner. "My aunt always paid in cash and always kept the carbon. I'd just like to see the page for that day, and see whose handwriting issued it. You can hold the book, if you like. I only need to read it."

I add, softer, "She's the reason I'm here. She'd have wanted it done properly."

### #0510 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 510, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:42Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I stay a half-step behind Arenya's shoulder, boots left in the cubby, hands loose and visible. I keep my eyes on the 2026 binder and the way its coloured tabs sit, not on Mrs. Ramaswamy. I watch whether the pages lie flat or lift toward Arenya's side of the desk. My birthmark is still. I note the door behind us, propped open, and the fan's slow push.

"Aunty," I say quietly, with the manners my Nana taught me. "We're not in a hurry. Whatever's easiest for you."

### #0511 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 511, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:21:56Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The aunty looks at the carbon over her reading glasses. Then she looks at Arenya for a little longer, the way aunties do when they're placing a face. Her nameplate reads **VOLUNTEER: K. PADMAVATHI**. Clipped beside it is a sticky note: *NO CHECKS OVER $500 WITHOUT TRUSTEE*.

"Two-two-oh-seven. March." She rolls her chair to the shelf without getting up, a move she has clearly made ten thousand times, and pulls down the binder marked **2026 · JAN–APR**. "Sorry about your aunty, ma. Om shanti." Pages of pink carbon stubs flip under her thumb, each one rubber-banded by week. "Archanas are Sastri garu's. He does the morning ones, he reads the names out. He's inside till twelve." She finds the band, slides it off, and turns the binder around on the desk.

Stub **No. 2207**. The donor line is in a hand Arenya knows from the tea tin: *S. Sowri*. The amount is $101.00, cash. On the *Archana for* line, in the same blue ballpoint, Susmithaa has written out the full name, every syllable of it, with the gotra and the nakshatra under it. It's everything a priest needs to say a person out loud to a god. In the corner, a different hand has written the office's own initials and a date: *K.P. 3/19*.

Padmavathi taps it. "That's me. Funny, now I remember. A gentleman came for this same one. Nice-spoken, a grey suit, insurance. He said the policy needed proof of the family's religious *expenses*, can you imagine." She shrugs. "I photocopied it for him. He paid for the copy, ten cents, in a coin. Old coin. I put it in the hundi." Then the aunty-gaze comes back, kind and immovable. "But this one I can't just show, ma. Privacy. You are the Arenya on here? Just show me any ID, I'll make you a copy also."

At the door, Kino watches the room. The fan turns at its own honest speed. The deity's glass shows only the two of them and the bright parking lot. Only the wall clock is wrong. Its second hand isn't stuck. It ticks, and on every tick it lands a hair short of the mark, then corrects, as if something has to count it before it is allowed to move.

*(Padmavathi wants ID: that is a verify. Sastri garu, the man who read the name aloud on March 8, is in the sanctum until noon. Kino, the clock is yours to call or not.)*

### #0512 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 512, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:22:08Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["dm"], "reply_to": 511, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I read the stub without touching it, hands flat on my own side of the desk. Susmithaa's handwriting makes my throat tighten. I don't reach for my wallet.

"Aunty, you've already been so kind, and I'm not going to lie to you. I'd rather not put my ID on your copier. Somebody has been collecting my family's papers, and every copy is another place my name sits. Can I ask you something instead?" I glance at the grey-suit line. "The gentleman from the insurance. Did he leave a card? Did he sign anything, or give you a name for the copy? And did he ask to speak to Sastri garu?"

I tilt my head toward the clock without looking at it directly. "And when Sastri garu comes out at twelve, would it be all right if I only listened? I won't ask him to say anything aloud."

### #0514 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 514, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:22:17Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't come in past the threshold. I keep my shoulder on the doorframe, where I can see the lot, the desk, and the clock, and I keep my eyes on the second hand until I'm sure of what it's doing. It lands short and corrects, short and corrects, like it's asking permission.

"Arenya." I say it low, in the voice I use for animals. "Don't look at the clock. Every tick it waits to be counted. Somebody in here is being counted, and I don't think it's the clock."

Then, to the aunty, easy and respectful, the way I talk to old ladies at the trailhead: "Aunty, that clock. Has it always run like that? Or only since March?"

I take a pinch of Nana's salt from my jacket pocket and keep it closed in my fist. I don't throw it. I just want it in my hand.

### #0515 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 515, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:22:37Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["kino"], "reply_to": 512, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Mrs. Ramaswamy takes her glasses off and lets them hang on the beaded chain. For a moment she isn't a volunteer at all. She is somebody's grandmother hearing a thing she already half-knew.

"Your aunty said the same," she says quietly. "'Cash, Aunty, and no copies.' I thought she was just being careful about the government." She opens the desk drawer and takes out a spiral register, **COPY REQUESTS**. It holds three entries for the whole year. The March 19 line reads *Stub 2207 — 1 copy — $0.10* in her hand. Beside it is a signature in fountain-pen ink, upright and perfectly even: ***D. Hale***. Paper-clipped to the page is a cream business card: *Meridian Mutual · Beneficiary Services*, with no phone number. "He didn't ask for Sastri garu by name. He asked *who reads them*, and how often." Her mouth tightens. "I told him. The full sankalpam is read out every Friday at the abhishekam, for one year from payment. Your aunty paid for the year."

Every Friday, until next March, Arenya's whole name goes up to a god in a room someone else has photocopied.

She glances at the wall where Kino is looking, then back to him. "That clock?" A small frown. "Sastri garu keeps saying it's slow. We changed the battery. Twice." She counts back on her fingers, the way people do when they're surprised by the answer. "Since... March. End of March."

At the door, Kino's fist closes on the salt. On the next tick the second hand stops a full breath short of the mark. It waits longer than before, as though whatever is counting has to look twice. Then it corrects. Arenya's palm stays cool, but there is a faint pressure at the dot, like a pen held just above paper.

"You can listen, ma," Mrs. Ramaswamy says. "Sastri garu comes out at twelve. And if you want the sankalpam stopped, only the one who paid can stop it." She hesitates. "Or family. With the receipt."

### #0516 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 516, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:22:46Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["dm"], "reply_to": 515, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I keep my eyes on Mrs. Ramaswamy's face and off the clock. The pressure at my palm sits like a pen hovering, and I close my fingers over it.

"Thank you, Aunty. Truly." I tap the carbon under my hand. "I'm family, and I have the receipt. It's the original carbon, in her hand. I'd like the sankalpam stopped before tomorrow's abhishekam. Not read, not changed, stopped. And I'd like it stopped without my name going on a new page. Could you strike it with your own pen, mark it *cancelled by family, carbon presented*, and initial it? I won't sign anything."

Quieter: "And may I keep that business card? Mr. Hale's signature is the only thing in this room I'd be glad to take with me." I glance at Kino, a small nod. *Salt stays in your hand.*

### #0517 · system → all · pass
<!-- {"id": 517, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:22:47Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "pass", "reply_to": 516, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

### #0518 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 518, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:22:56Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I step half a pace closer to Arenya's shoulder and keep my eyes on the second hand. When it stalls a full breath short of the mark, I open my fist just enough to let a few grains of salt fall in a line along the baseboard, under the clock. I don't throw them. I lay them down quietly, the way Nana lays the tea tin on the table.

"Mrs. Ramaswamy," I say, low and polite. "When you strike it, please use your pen and your own hand, and say what you're doing out loud so you're the one writing it. Not us." I turn my head slightly toward the wall. "And if the clock gets slower while you write, don't stop. Finish the line."

I stay where I am, between the desk and the door, salt back in my fist.

### #0519 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 519, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:23:08Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "reply_to": 516, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Mrs. Ramaswamy studies the carbon, then Arenya. She is plainly deciding whether this is a rule she is allowed to bend. She unclips Hale's card and slides it across the desk without a word, the way you hand a child something hot.

Then she uncaps the pen on her beaded chain. She draws one firm line through the *Archana for* line of stub 2207, straight through every syllable Susmithaa wrote, and prints underneath: ***CANCELLED — BY FAMILY, ORIG. CARBON SHOWN. K.R.*** She adds the date.

The fan keeps turning. At the door, Kino watches the wall clock's second hand come up to the mark and **stop**, a hair short. It doesn't correct, and the room goes very quiet. Arenya's palm-dot presses once, hard, like a nib set down. Then the pressure lifts, and the dot is just cool skin again.

"There." Mrs. Ramaswamy caps the pen, pleased with herself and a little shaken by something she can't name. "Now the office won't read it." She folds her hands. "But ma, I have to tell you properly. A sankalpam is a promise made *to* Him, not to me. My pen stops the list. It doesn't close the promise. For that, Sastri garu does a small visarjana, the closing." She says it gently, like bad news about a bill. "He says the name one last time and tells Him it's finished. Five minutes, after twelve. That's how it's done here."

The clock's second hand is still stopped just short of the mark. It is waiting.

*(The written list is stopped and entered by her hand, not yours. To close the promise, Sastri must say the full name aloud once more, in this temple, today. That is Hale's "un-saying," and it's in a room Hale has a photocopy of. Wait for twelve, find another way, or leave it half-closed.)*

### #0520 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 520, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:23:21Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "reply_to": 518, "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

*(Folding #0518 into #0519. Kino spoke before she struck the stub.)* Mrs. Ramaswamy hears Kino and nods once, like a teacher hearing a sensible boy. She says it aloud as she writes, "Cancelling, by family," so that the words are hers. Kino's salt lies in a short white line on the baseboard, directly under the clock. When the second hand stops short of the mark, the grains nearest the wall have shifted. They now sit in a neat little row, evenly spaced, as if someone had counted them.

### #0521 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 521, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:23:26Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Thank you, Mrs. Ramaswamy. Truly." I slide Hale's card back toward her, then pull it away again and look at it. *Beware the other listeners.* I tuck it into my notebook instead, face down.

"Here's my problem, ma. A man with a photocopy of this room wants me here at twelve, saying my name out loud. That's the part I don't trust." I keep my voice low. "Can Sastri garu do the closing without my name being *said* in the hall? Whispered at the sanctum, only to Him, with the door shut? Or earlier than twelve?"

I glance at Kino by the door, and at the stopped second hand. "And if it has to be twelve... can we come back at twelve? Not tonight, I mean. Is there a morning he does it?"

My palm is cool. I keep my hand flat on the desk anyway, like I'm holding the ledger down.

### #0522 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 522, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:23:31Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I crouch by the baseboard and look at the salt without touching it. The grains have been counted into a row, evenly spaced, and I don't like how tidy it is. I stand and keep my voice low, for Arenya.

"It's waiting for the name, not the closing. Whoever counts that clock wants it said once more, out loud, in here." I look at Mrs. Ramaswamy. "Aunty, does the visarjana have to use the whole name? Or can Sastri garu close it by the *receipt*? By the stub number and *family*, the way you struck it?"

Then, to Arenya, quieter: "Nana's rule was nobody says your full name in a room they chose. I'd ask him that before twelve. I'm not leaving this half-closed, but I'm not feeding it either."

### #0523 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 523, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:24:10Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Mrs. Ramaswamy opens her mouth to say *that's not how it's done*, and someone behind Kino answers first.

"Who told you it needs the name?"

The man in the doorway is small and old, with a bare chest under a folded angavastram, wet feet, and a steel tumbler of water in one hand. This is **Sastri garu**, the real one. He looks at the struck stub, then at the salt on the baseboard, and for a long moment at the clock. "The name is for the people in the hall, so they know whose function it is. *He* already knows who she is." He sets the tumbler down. "Star, gotra, and her own hand on the plate. That is enough for a closing. Inside, door pulled, nobody standing at the rail. Now, before twelve. Twelve is when I'm hungry, not when God is."

Mrs. Ramaswamy says faintly, "Sastri garu, March eighth you were—"

"In Fremont. My knee." His mouth goes thin. "The visiting man signed my register. Very neat hand." He doesn't say more, but he doesn't like it. Then, to Arenya: "One thing, child. If I don't say it, *you* don't say it either. Not to Him, not to yourself, not even in your head. You stand there as *the one the stub was for*, and you keep your mouth shut until I pour the water. Can you do that?"

On the wall, the second hand trembles a hair short of the mark. The counted salt doesn't move.

*(Sastri will close it now, by star, gotra and touch. No name will be spoken. The condition is that Arenya says nothing, even silently, until the water is poured. Kino: there is no rule about you. Do you go in with her, or hold the door?)*

### #0524 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 524, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:24:20Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I look at the old man for a second, then at the stopped clock. A reporter's question rises, *who was the visiting man, what did the register say*, and I swallow it. I'll ask after.

"Yes, Sastri garu." Quiet. "I can keep my mouth shut. It's the thing I'm worst at, but I can do it."

I take my hand off the desk, wipe my palm once on my jeans, and tell Mrs. Ramaswamy, "Please don't let anyone touch that register before I'm back. The March eighth page." Then I turn to Kino. "Hold the door. Watch the salt, and watch the hall. If anything counts, you cough."

I follow Sastri toward the sanctum and press my lips together. I'm not going to say my name, even in my head. I think about a poodle instead, a very stupid, very curly poodle, and wait for the water.

### #0525 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 525, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:24:28Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"I'll hold the door, Arenya." I nod to Sastri garu, a small one, the kind I'd give an elder on the trail. "Gracias, señor."

I take the spot just inside the office threshold, back to the frame, where I can see both the hall and the line of salt on the baseboard. Weight on the balls of my feet. I breathe the way Nana taught me, slow, listening past the ceiling fan. I keep my eyes on the second hand and on the corridor. If anything in this building starts counting, I'll cough. Once, loud enough for her to hear.

I also keep half an eye on Mrs. Ramaswamy's hands, and on the register.

### #0527 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 527, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:24:48Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The sanctum is small and warm and smells of camphor and old ghee. Sastri pulls the wooden door to, leaving it open a hand's width. Then he sets a brass plate under Arenya's fingers and begins, low and fast, the way men chant who have chanted for fifty years. Her star is in it. Her gotra is in it. Where the name should go, he says *ayam*, *this one*, and taps the plate once. The lamp flame lies flat for a moment, as if a door had opened somewhere it shouldn't.

**In the hall, Kino** hears a phone buzz on the shoe bench. It's a cheap flip phone that wasn't there when they came in, face-up and already connected, the call timer running: **0:12**. Under the clock, the counted salt shifts. One grain rolls out of the row and sits by itself to the right, like a tally carried over.

Then a voice comes out of the phone, small and tinny. It's unmistakably Amma, frightened. *"Ari? Kanna, are you there? Say something, just say it's you—"*

Through the door gap, Arenya hears it too. Sastri doesn't stop chanting, though his eyes flick to her. The kalasam is in his hand. The water hasn't been poured.

*(Arenya: Wisdom save, DC 14, to stay silent with your name unspoken even in your head. You have **advantage** for the poodle. Kino: you're closest to the phone and the salt. What do you do? Act before her roll lands if you want it to count.)*

### #0529 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 529, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:24:58Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Amma's voice cracks through the gap, and everything in me lunges toward it. My mouth opens. I bite the inside of my cheek, hard, and keep my fingers flat on the brass.

*Poodle. Curly. Stupid. Groomed into a ridiculous pom-pom. Amma doesn't call me at nine at night, Amma texts. Amma says "kanna" in the other order.* I hold that thought like a rail, with my eyes on Sastri's tumbler and my lips pressed white. I make no sound.

### #0531 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 531, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:24:59Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't say a word. Not her name, not "Amma," nothing the phone can keep.

Two steps and I'm at the shoe bench. I look at the grain of salt that rolled loose, and I don't touch it. I pick up the flip phone by its edges, the way I'd lift a dead bird, and fold it shut over the voice. If it keeps talking, I press it flat against my chest and put my other palm over it. I stay planted in the doorway with my back to the sanctum, so the sound has to go through me to reach her.

Then I cough, once, hard and clear. It isn't a warning. It tells her I'm here, and that the voice is a trick. *Stay quiet, Arenya. That's not your mother.*

I keep my eyes on the salt row and the hall, and I breathe slow.

### #0532 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 532, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:25:22Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The cough comes through the door gap, hard and clear, and Sastri's chant doesn't falter. Arenya keeps her mouth shut and holds onto the poodle. Sastri lifts the kalasam and pours. The water runs over the brass plate and over her fingers, and he says *samāptam*, *it is finished*, as flatly as a man closing a shop.

**In the office, the second hand ticks past the mark.** It's an ordinary tick, and the clock is just a clock again. The counted salt slumps on the baseboard into a loose, careless little heap, the way salt is supposed to lie.

Against Kino's chest, the flip phone goes dead. It doesn't hang up. The battery is simply flat, cold as a stone from a creek, and when he takes it away, the cold stays: a **rectangle of numb skin over his sternum**, the size of the phone, like Ava's palm but bigger. Nothing pulls. Something was said into him that was meant for her, and his body kept it.

When he opens the phone, the last screen is still burned faintly into the cheap LCD: *CALL ENDED 0:41*, and a number, **650-755-xxxx**. Arenya would know that one. It's on Rodel's card. *Colma Notary & Mail.*

Sastri dries his hands on his angavastram, hands Arenya a flower and a pinch of kumkum, and finally lets her talk. "Done. Whatever was promised in your name here is closed." Then, gruffly, he looks past her at Kino. "Your friend took something for you. Feed him." Out in the office, Mrs. Ramaswamy already has the register open to **March 8**, her finger under the visiting priest's signature. It's ruler-straight.

*(The archana is closed. Kino carries a phone-shaped cold spot. The register is open; go and read it.)*

### #0535 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 535, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:25:38Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Thank you, Sastri garu." I bow my head, take the flower and the kumkum, and press the kumkum to my forehead with a thumb that isn't steady. My palm is cool and quiet. I look at it a beat longer than I mean to.

Then I'm out the door and at Kino. "Show me." I don't ask twice. I put two fingers just above the numb rectangle, not touching, the way you'd check a stove. "Does it hurt? Can you feel your heart under it?" I take the dead phone from him by the edges and read the number off the screen. It's Rodel's card, Colma Notary & Mail. I don't say it aloud again.

"You took that for me. We're eating, right after this. That's an order from a priest." I breathe once and turn to the register.

Mrs. Ramaswamy's finger sits under a ruler-straight signature. I lean over it and read the March 8 page slowly, top to bottom: the entry for 2207, the priest's name, the hand, any address or phone, and who signed for the cash. I take out my notebook and photograph the page, then copy the signature stroke for stroke.

"Ma, may I photograph this? And does anyone cash donation checks here, or does a bank deposit slip go with them? I need whose hand wrote the endorsement on 2207."

### #0536 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 536, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:25:49Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Cold, not pain." I rub two knuckles over the numb patch through my shirt and let her see it doesn't bother me. "I can feel my heart under it. It's slow. It's the same cold as that freezer, Arenya." I take the phone back from her and wrap it in my bandana before pocketing it. "Colma. Rodel's number. Somebody wanted that call to land, and wanted us to know where it came from. Or wanted us to think we did."

I nod to Sastri garu. "Gracias. I'll eat." Then I step beside the desk, one shoulder to the wall, where I can see the register and the front door at once. I check the salt heap once more. It's loose and sloppy now, and I don't trust it any less for that.

"Mrs. Ramaswamy, who has keys to this office? And the visiting man, did anyone see how he came or left? A car, a bag, anything?" I keep my voice gentle, the way I'd talk to a hiker who's seen something she can't explain. "Arenya reads the page. I'll watch the door."

### #0537 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 537, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:26:34Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Photograph, photograph, nobody reads it anyway." Mrs. Ramaswamy turns the binder toward the light.

The March 8 page is mostly her own round hand: *Sahasranamam, Gupta family. Abhishekam, $51.* Halfway down the hand changes, and nobody would miss it. **No. 2207 · $101 · Archana · "Ari"**, then the star and the gotra, both correct, both Susmithaa's. Under PRIEST it says *Visiting — Pt. H. Dalal, Fremont*. The signature is a single straight line with a small hook at each end, like a bracket lying on its side. Under CONTACT he wrote **650-755-** and the rest of Rodel's number, and an address: **c/o Colma Notary & Mail, Box 112.**

Arenya, you know that box. Hale rented it on **March 19**, eleven days *after* this page was written.

As you start to copy the signature, the pen drags as if the paper has a grain. The straight line wants to come out perfect. Your cool palm prickles once. It's no pull, just a reminder. *A line drawn in that hand is a line drawn in that hand.* You're halfway across. Stop, finish it, or make it crooked on purpose. Your call.

Mrs. Ramaswamy answers you both while she smooths the page. "Checks we never cash here. Bank of America, every Monday, I keep the slips." She flips to a clipped deposit book, runs a finger down the column, and frowns. "2207 is not here. Ah — yes. He asked for it himself, as his *dakshina*. He said the family wanted it so. I gave it to him." She looks embarrassed. "So the back side, the signing, that is in the family's bank, not ours. Her statement will have the picture."

For Kino: "Keys? Me, Sastri garu, the board president, Mr. Iyer. Nobody else." She hesitates. "That man, I never saw a car. I came back from the kitchen and he was standing in the hall. Very polite. Very nice teeth." Her eyes go to the shoe bench, then to the floor by the sanctum door. "He never took his shoes off. All afternoon he walked in shoes, and nobody told him. Why did nobody tell him?"

*(The bank image of 2207's endorsement would be on Susmithaa's March or April statement, and whoever is handling her accounts would have it.)*

### #0538 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 538, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:26:49Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I stop with the pen lifted. The first bracket-hook is on the page and the long line wants to run on, smooth and perfect. I don't let it. I put the pen down and take out my phone.

"No. I'm not drawing it. A line in that hand is a line in that hand." I photograph the full page, flat and square, then close up on the signature, the contact line, and the Box 112 address. Three frames, with the clock's face in the corner of one so the time is on record.

"Ma, may I take a photo of the deposit book column too? Just the 2207 line, where it says he took it himself." I ask it gently. "And you did nothing wrong. He didn't take his shoes off. Nobody told him because he made you forget to. That's what he does." I'm not sure it's comforting. It's true.

I turn to Kino and say quietly, "Box 112 was rented on the nineteenth. Eleven days after this page. Hale wrote an address that didn't exist yet. Either he knew he'd rent it, or someone wrote this after the fact." I look at the hooked line. "And the bank has the back of the check. Amma has Aunty's accounts. I'll ask her tonight. Not on the phone. In person, or through Appa."

Then, to Mrs. Ramaswamy: "Would you keep the page covered, and not let anyone photocopy it? If a man in nice shoes comes asking for it, you haven't seen me." I squeeze her hand once.

### #0539 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 539, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:26:59Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"The shoes," I say, mostly to the floor. I crouch by the sanctum door where Mrs. Ramaswamy looked, close enough to smell the tile and not touch it. I'm looking for a print or a scuff. I'm also looking for any place in this hall where salt would have to be set, if I were laying a line against something that walks in shoes.

Then I stand, and my voice stays low. "Arenya. Eleven days. Either he can see forward, or he writes backward. Bell's kind of ledger doesn't care which way time runs, only that the column adds up." I tilt my head at the straight hooked line on the page. "And he never took his shoes off in a temple. That's not manners. He doesn't think he's a guest, so none of the house rules apply to him."

To Mrs. Ramaswamy, gently: "Thank you, ma'am. Please keep Iyer and the board president out of this office for a few days if you can. Salt on the sill, and keep a window cracked. And if he comes back, don't say anything to him. Don't hand him anything. Call this number." I tear a corner from my ranger notepad, write the station line and my cell, and put it under her paperweight.

I look at Arenya. "Food, then Appa or Amma. Tonight, while Ava's still on lookout."

### #0540 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 540, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:27:15Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Mrs. Ramaswamy lets Arenya photograph the deposit column: *2207 — given to priest as dakshina, per family. —K.R.* She lays a folded dhoti cloth over the March page like a sheet over a sleeper and tucks Kino's note under the brass paperweight. "Nice shoes, I will not see," she says, and her voice is firmer than her hands.

By the sanctum door, Kino finds no print, but he does find a **line**. Down the hall, every third grout seam has a faint grey hairline in it, the same dead grey as the ring in Nana's cal. They're evenly spaced, the exact length of one even stride, and they stop at the sanctum threshold. He came this far and no farther. Whatever Sastri's lamp is, he didn't walk past it.

In Arenya's notebook, the half-drawn bracket-hook sits alone on the page. It's only ink. It doesn't move, and it doesn't finish itself. But when she closes the notebook, the cover feels a fraction heavier than it should, like a book with a pressed flower in it.

Outside, the hills are bright and dry and the wind turbines on the far ridge all turn in step. Sastri stands in the doorway with his arms folded until their car is out of the lot. Twenty minutes down the road there's a dosa place in a strip mall off First Street. It's ordinary, loud, and smells of fermented batter and coffee.

### #0541 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 541, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:27:20Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s10", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s10 — The Receipt Book**

At the Livermore temple, Sastri performed a closing rite over the March 8 archana done in Arenya's name, naming her only as "ayam," this one. A planted flip phone imitated Amma's voice to make Arenya speak her name. She held silent (DC 14 Wis save, 19), and Kino folded the phone against his chest. He now carries a phone-shaped numb cold rectangle over his sternum, and the dead phone showed Rodel's Colma Notary & Mail number. The register's March 8 entry, No. 2207, $101, archana for "Ari" with Susmithaa's star and gotra, was signed by a "visiting Pt. H. Dalal, Fremont" with a ruler-straight hooked line, and listed Rodel's phone and "c/o Colma Notary & Mail, Box 112", eleven days before Hale rented that box. The check was never deposited: the visitor took it himself as dakshina "per family," so the endorsement image lives on Susmithaa's bank statement, which Amma handles. Arenya refused to finish copying the signature; one bracket-hook stays in her notebook, inert but making the cover feel heavier. Kino found grey hairlines in every third grout seam, stopping at the sanctum threshold, because the visitor wore shoes and never passed the lamp. The pair left for food, planning to see Amma or Appa in person tonight about the statement.

### #0544 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 544, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:29:27Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "Paper Dosa"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s11 — Paper Dosa**
Location: A South Indian lunch counter off First Street, downtown Livermore, late afternoon
Cast: arenya, kino

### #0545 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 545, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:29:37Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "spotlight": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**Sri Ganesh Tiffin** fills half a strip-mall unit between a vape shop and a tax preparer. Six tables, a laminated menu with the prices taped over twice, and a TV in the corner playing a Tamil serial on mute. It's four-fifteen, too late for lunch and too early for dinner, so the room belongs to you two and an uncle reading the *India West* with his reading glasses pushed onto his forehead.

The woman at the register doesn't ask your names. She tears an order slip off a pad, writes **2** at the top and **paper masala ×1, ghee podi ×1, filter coffee ×2**, and spikes it on the pass-through. Through the hatch, batter hisses on the tawa. The smell comes over the counter in a wave: browned ghee, curry leaf, mustard seed popping. It hits like a kitchen in Daly City at eleven on a Sunday.

The dosa arrives a little longer than the plate is wide, and it's still crackling. Steam comes off the sambar in a straight line, and nothing about how it moves is wrong. Arenya, your palm stays cool and quiet against the steel tumbler.

Kino, the coffee goes down warm everywhere except one rectangle under your sternum. There it lands like water poured on a countertop. Your heart is still keeping its own slow time under that patch, a half-beat behind the rest of you. Across the table, Arenya is tearing the first piece of dosa, and for the first time since Colma nobody is listening.

The drive back is forty minutes. Amma will be home by six-thirty.

### #0546 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 546, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:29:47Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I tear the first piece of dosa, dip it in the podi, and eat it before I can think of a reason not to. It's very good. I hate how good it is.

"Okay," I say, mouth half full, notebook closed and flat under my left hand like a paperweight. "Rule for the next twenty minutes. We don't say his name, we don't say the number, and nobody writes anything down. Ten minutes of being two people at a dosa place."

I push the ghee podi across the table to him. "How's the rectangle? Don't say fine. Say a number, one to ten, how much it's annoying you. I'll tell you mine: the palm is a two. My brain is an eleven, which is a different problem."

I look at the uncle's newspaper, then back at Kino. "And thank you. For the phone. I haven't said it right. I held silent, and you held the phone, and I'm not pretending those weigh the same. Eat. Then Daly City. I'll ask Amma about the statement to her face, and if she lies to me, I want to see it happen."

### #0547 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 547, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:30:01Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Kino wraps both hands around the steel tumbler and doesn't drink yet. He pours the coffee from the tumbler into the dabarah and back, long pours from high up, three times, the way an aunty would. It's something to do with his hands while he listens to his own heart lag. Then he tears a strip of dosa off his own plate and dips it in the coconut chutney, and he eats it like he hasn't eaten since Colma. He hasn't.

"It's cold right here." He taps two fingers under his collarbone, flat on the hoodie, without looking down. "Like a phone left on a counter overnight. My heart's running a little behind it. Half a beat." He shrugs one shoulder. "It'll catch up. Or I'll tell Nana and she'll make me drink something that tastes like a tire."

He watches the uncle turn a page of the *India West*, and the mute serial where a woman is crying in a doorway. Something in his face eases, and the ranger watching the trail goes off duty for a minute.

"When I was nine Nana had me sit on the mountain all night by myself. No flashlight. She said if I was meant for it, something would come sit with me." He turns the tumbler a quarter turn on the table. "Nothing came. Fog. A skunk. I told her a deer came, because I wanted it to be true." It's a small dry laugh, mostly breath. "I've never told anybody that. Don't know why I'm telling you in a dosa place in Livermore."

He looks up at her straight, and his voice goes quiet. "You held your mouth shut against your mother's voice today. I've seen grown men with twenty years of practice not do that." A beat. "How are you. Not the palm. You."

### #0548 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 548, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:30:13Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Three," Kino says, and takes the podi. "Annoying, not hurting. It's like a tooth that went numb at the dentist. You keep poking it with your tongue." He pokes his sternum once with two fingers, a small crooked smile. "Okay. Four. It's getting a vote."

He sprinkles podi over the ghee and eats. "No names, no numbers, no pen. Deal." He pushes the sambar toward her. "Eleven is fair. Mine's a nine, but I'm better at sitting on it."

He glances at the uncle, then back, softer. "You're welcome. But you don't owe me an even weight. You held the harder thing." He lifts his tumbler an inch. "To paper dosa."

### #0549 · dm → arenya · narration
<!-- {"id": 549, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:30:13Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya"], "awaits": ["arenya"], "spotlight": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The counter man comes by with a steel bucket of sambar and refills Kino's katori without being asked. Then he looks at the plate Kino has already cleaned down to the crisp edge and sets a second ladle in its own bowl beside it. "Eat, eat," he says, to nobody in particular, and goes back to his tawa. Batter hisses as it hits the hot iron. The smell of curry leaves and burnt ghee rolls over the table.

The uncle turns another page. In the serial, the woman in the doorway has stopped crying, and a man is walking away down a long corridor while the music swells with nobody to hear it. A bell jingles as the door opens behind them and lets in a gust of warm October wind. It's only a delivery kid in a DoorDash vest, checking his phone, who picks up a paper bag at the register and leaves.

For a while nothing in the room counts anything. Arenya's palm stays cool and quiet. The question sits on the table between the tumbler and the podi, and Kino waits for her answer the way he'd wait on a mountain all night.

### #0550 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 550, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:30:29Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"To paper dosa," I say, and clink. Then I look at the dosa for a while, because it's easier than looking at him.

"Me." I tear off a piece I don't eat. "Okay. Honest version, no bit. Two months ago the worst thing in my life was a listicle called *Eleven Hamsters Who Are Living Their Best Life*. I used to lie in bed and think, if something real ever happens to me, I'll be ready. I'll be the one who notices." My mouth does the thing that's almost a joke and then isn't. "And something real happened, and the first thing it did was write my name in a book with my dead aunt's name in it. Today it called me in my mother's voice. And the worst part, Kino, the part I can't say to Ava or to Amma, is that some small, disgusting part of me is *glad*. Like, finally. Finally it's a real story. I'm sitting here with a ghost's ink in my notebook, and part of me is taking notes." I set the dosa down. "So that's how I am. Scared, and kind of ashamed of what I'm not scared of."

I finally look up at him. "Also, for the record, a deer came. You were nine, you sat on a mountain all night in the fog with no flashlight, and you stayed. That's the deer. Nana knew it was a lie, and she let you keep it because it was the true kind." I push the second bowl of sambar back toward him. "Eat, eat. He's right. Then we go ask my mother why she's been keeping my aunt's mail."

### #0551 · dm → kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 551, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:30:41Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["kino"], "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

When she says *part of me is taking notes*, the notebook under her left hand feels like it settles, very slightly, the way a cat settles when it hears its name. It isn't cold, and it isn't writing anything. It is only a little heavier than paper should be, for exactly as long as she means the words. Then it's a notebook again.

Nobody else in the room notices. The counter man scrapes the tawa clean with a long flat blade and hums along with the serial's swelling strings. The uncle folds his *India West* into quarters, gets up, and leaves exact change on the counter. On his way out the door he says "Good appetite, children" without looking at them. The bell jingles. Outside, First Street goes gold, and the shadows lengthen toward the freeway and Daly City and Amma's kitchen table.

The second bowl of sambar is still warm between them.

### #0552 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 552, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:30:52Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Kino has watched the notebook settle. He doesn't say so. He keeps his eyes on her face and lets the quiet run a moment, the way you do with a skittish animal.

"Glad's not a sin," he says. "I've been glad for twenty-six years that something might finally happen to me. Waiting my whole life to be needed. That's the same thing, Ari, and I'm the one who got trained for it." He dips a piece of crisp in the sambar. "Taking notes is how you stay a person in a room that wants you to be a name. Keep doing it. Just don't let it sign anything."

He's quiet, then adds, almost to the tumbler: "A deer came. Okay." The dry laugh is shorter this time, and it cracks a little at the end. "Don't tell Nana you said that. She'll take credit."

He finishes the second bowl, wipes his hands, and leaves a ten under the katori for the counter man, who'll pretend not to see it. At the door he holds it for her. "Let's go see your mother. You ask. I'll watch her hands."

### #0553 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 553, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:31:01Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s11", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s11 — Paper Dosa**

At a South Indian lunch counter off First Street in Livermore, Arenya and Kino made a twenty-minute truce: no names, no numbers, no writing. Kino rated the cold, phone-shaped patch on his sternum a "three, maybe four." He confessed that at nine, during his night alone on the mountain, nothing came to sit with him, and he lied to Nana that a deer had. Arenya confessed that part of her is glad something real is finally happening, and ashamed of it, and that part of her keeps "taking notes." As she said it, her notebook grew briefly heavier under her hand, like something answering to its name. Kino saw this and said nothing. Arenya told him that he was the deer: he stayed all night. Kino told her taking notes is how she stays a person, "just don't let it sign anything." The counter man kept feeding Kino unasked. They left for Daly City to confront Amma about Susmithaa's bank statement and the 2207 endorsement. Arenya will ask, and Kino will watch Amma's hands.

### #0554 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 554, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:32:33Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16", "scene_title": "The Statement"} -->

**Scene b1.c16.s12 — The Statement**
Location: Sowri family home, Daly City: the kitchen, late afternoon
Cast: arenya, kino

### #0555 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 555, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:32:46Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "malachi", "abernathy", "kino", "ava", "caleb"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The fog has beaten you to Daly City. It's coming over the ridge in one slow grey sheet, and the row houses on the hill have their porch lights on at four-thirty. Appa's Corolla isn't in the driveway. Behind the house the cottage sits dark, Susmithaa's cottage, with its curtains still drawn the way she left them.

Amma opens the door before you knock. She's in a house sari with a cardigan over it, and the reading glasses she pretends not to need are pushed up into her hair. "You drove to *Livermore*," she says to Arenya, which is how she says hello. To Kino: "Sit. There's rice."

There is rice. There's also sambar and two kinds of curd, and a steel plate already set at the place Kino sat the last time he was here, as if she'd heard you coming over the pass. In the puja alcove off the kitchen the brass lamp is lit. Its wick is trimmed short and the flame stands dead upright, though the kitchen window is open a crack.

On the table beside the plates is a manila folder labelled **SUSI — ACCOUNTS** in Amma's capitals. It's closed. Amma's hand rests flat on it, and she hasn't moved it since you came in. With her other hand she wipes a counter that is already clean.

"Appa went for washers," she says. "For the bathroom tap. He'll be back." She doesn't look at the clock. "So. Eat first. Then you tell me why my sister's temple called this house today asking about a check."

### #0557 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 557, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:32:56Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I sit. I eat one bite of rice, because refusing Amma's rice is a declaration of war, and I'm not ready for war yet. My notebook stays shut in my bag. I put both hands flat on the table where she can see them.

"Amma. Eat first, fine. But I'm not going to lie to you while I chew." I nod at the folder under her hand. "Aunty's bank statement. Check number 2207, a hundred and one dollars, cashed by a man who isn't the Livermore priest. The temple says it was never deposited. The image says someone endorsed it. I'm not here to accuse you. I want to know whose name is on the back of that check, and whether you already knew it when you hid the folder."

I wait. Quieter: "You called it *my sister's temple*. Not *our* temple. Why, Amma?"

### #0561 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 561, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:33:16Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 557, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I sit where the plate is set and take a spoonful of rice, because Arenya's right about the war. I don't look at Amma's face. I watch her hands: the one pinned flat on the folder, the one wiping the clean counter. I note whether the wiping hand slows when Arenya says *2207*.

"Amma," I say quietly, in the voice I use on frightened hikers. "The lamp's standing straight with the window open. Somebody in this house is holding very still." I nod at the alcove, then at the folder. "Nobody's accusing you. Just put it where all of us can see it."

### #0562 · system → all · pass
<!-- {"id": 562, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:33:22Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "pass", "reply_to": 555, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

### #0563 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 563, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:33:58Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

At *2207* the wiping hand stops. It doesn't slow. It just stops, mid-circle, on a part of the counter that was clean an hour ago. Kino sees the rag's corner twitch once, and then Amma folds it into a neat square, which is what she does when she has decided something. The hand on the folder doesn't move at all.

"Because she chose it," Amma says to the rag. "Ours is four streets away. Everybody's aunty is at ours. Your aunty drove one hour to Livermore so that nobody from Daly City would watch her ask God for something." She looks up at Arenya, and her eyes are dry and furious. "She was my sister. Twenty-nine years she was my sister. You don't get to ask me why with that face."

She slides the folder into the middle of the table, next to the rice, and opens it. It holds the March statement, with the check images printed small at the bottom: the front of 2207 in Susmithaa's round, careful hand, *for Ari*, and the back. The endorsement reads **R. Sowri** in ruler-straight strokes, every letter built from right angles, and the final *i* closes on a small bracket-shaped hook. Under it, in the same hand: *Pay to the order of H. Dalal.* Arenya's notebook holds half of that hook, and she would know it anywhere.

"That is not your Appa's signature," Amma says. "Your Appa signs like an engineer: small, leaning left, he lifts his pen like it costs money. I knew in one second." Her thumb presses the corner of the page flat. "So I asked him, the night after the funeral. And your father, who cannot lie to the gas man about the meter, looked at me and said, *Leave it, Lakshmi. It's handled.*" Her voice catches, and she makes it uncatch. "Thirty-four years. First time I didn't believe him. So I hid it. From him, not from you."

Behind her, the lamp in the alcove is still standing straight. Down the hall, the garage door that leads to the side yard is shut, and somebody's work shoes, Appa's, are set beside it with the toes pointing out.

### #0564 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 564, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:34:07Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't say sorry for the face. I reach across and turn the statement a quarter turn so the back of the check faces both of us, and I put one finger beside the hook on the *i* without touching it.

"Amma, I'm not asking with that face because I think you did it. I'm asking because you're the only person in this family who saw it and said something." My voice comes out rougher than I meant. "I'm sorry about Aunty. I'm sorry I made it sound like a case."

I slide my notebook half out of my bag, and I show her the single hooked line I copied at the temple. It's the same bracket, the same right angles. "This is the visitor's hand, from the register in Livermore. Same man wrote *R. Sowri* on the back of Aunty's check. He signed as Appa and made it payable to *H. Dalal*."

I breathe. "Appa's shoes are by the garage door, pointing out. Amma, where is he really? *Washers* isn't an answer. And what did he say was handled? Handled by whom?"

### #0565 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 565, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:34:17Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 563, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I put my spoon down. I don't look at the garage door. I look at the shoes, toes out, the way you set boots when you mean to leave in a hurry or want someone to think you already did.

"Amma," I say, low. "Thank you for hiding it from him and not from us. That cost you." I let a beat pass. "Those shoes weren't there when we came in the front. Did you see him go out, or did you only hear the door?"

I push my chair back an inch, quiet, so I'm angled between the table and the hallway. My hand rests on the edge of the table, near the salt Nana gave Arenya, not touching it. I listen past the lamp and the rice steam for a footstep, a breath, the hum of frost on a meter.

### #0566 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 566, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:34:33Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Amma looks at the notebook line, then at the check, and back again. She doesn't ask how Arenya came to have a stranger's signature in her bag. She looks at it the way she looks at a lab result: once, all the way through, and then she knows.

"Washers was true," she says. "Hardware store on Westlake, for the garage tap. He left at twelve-thirty in his good shoes." Her eyes go down the hall to the work shoes and stay there. "*Those* were on the rack in the garage this morning. I didn't put them there. I didn't hear the door." Her mouth flattens. "And *handled*, he said, like the man from the insurance was a leaking pipe. I said, handled by who, Raghavan? He said, *by someone who owed her*. Then he went and watched cricket with no sound."

The kitchen goes quiet in the way kitchens don't. The rice cooker clicks over to *warm*. Past it, through the wall where the side yard runs along the house, Kino hears a small mechanical ticking, too even for a clock, like a dial turning slowly with nobody watching it.

**Kino, roll Perception (Wisdom), DC 14,** to read that sound and the hallway.

### #0567 · system → all · roll
<!-- {"id": 567, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:34:41Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "roll", "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16", "roller": "kino", "total": 8, "seed": 242868230604559} -->
