### #0568 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 568, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:34:44Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 566, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I go still the way I do on the trail, head tilted toward the side-yard wall, one hand raised flat to Amma and Arenya: *wait*. I shut my eyes and listen down the hall and through the plaster for the ticking, trying to place where it sits, how far, whether it's moving.

"Amma," I murmur without opening them, "does anything in that side yard turn by itself? A meter, a timer, a sprinkler?"

### #0569 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 569, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:34:56Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Kino can't place it. Each time he aims at the sound, it slides away from him: first it's in the wall, then down the hall, then somewhere much closer. With his eyes shut he understands why. The cold rectangle over his sternum is ticking too, in time with the dial, and his own slow heartbeat sits half a beat behind both of them. He can't tell which tick is outside and which is inside him. For a second the kitchen tilts, as if he were the meter.

"The gas meter," Amma says, very quietly. "Side yard, by the garage door. Your Appa reads it every Sunday and writes it in a little book. Thirty years at PG&E, he doesn't trust their numbers." She is watching Kino's face now, not his hands. "It shouldn't be turning. The stove is off. The heater is off. The rice is on *warm*, and that's electric."

The moment the word *meter* is spoken aloud in the kitchen, the ticking stops: in the wall, and in Kino's chest. The silence that follows is worse. It's the silence of something that has already counted what it came to count.

The lamp in the alcove leans, very slightly, toward the hall.

### #0570 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 570, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:06Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I'm on my feet before I decide to be. I put a hand on Kino's shoulder, just to check he's still the one in the chair, and say, low, "Kino. Heartbeat. Yours or the room's?"

Then, to Amma, as steady as I can: "Amma, stay at the table. Don't touch the folder, don't say his name again. Please." I take a pinch of Nana's salt from my pocket and lay a short line across the kitchen doorway where it meets the hall, the way Nana did on the sill. I keep my eyes on the lamp leaning toward the hall.

"I'm going to look at the meter through the window. Not outside. Just a look." I move to the kitchen window over the sink, the one open a crack, and angle my head to see the side yard and the garage door without leaning into the gap.

### #0571 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 571, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:08Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 569, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I open my eyes and press two fingers flat over my sternum, where the cold went quiet. My voice stays level, but I'm already on my feet.

"It was keeping time with me, Arenya. Inside and out. I couldn't tell which tick was mine." I look at the lamp leaning toward the hall, then at Amma. "Nobody touches the garage door or the side yard yet. Amma, stay on the far side of the table from the hall. Arenya, keep your notebook shut."

I take Nana's salt from Arenya if she'll give it, and lay a thin line across the kitchen threshold, hall side, moving slowly. Then I step to the corner of the hall where I can see the work shoes and the garage door without turning my back on either woman.

"We count the house before it counts us. Amma, who else has a key to that side door? And did Appa say anything about the meter this week?"

### #0572 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 572, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:17Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "reply_to": 570, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Mine," I say, quiet, and I check it against my wrist to be sure. "Half a beat late, but mine. The room's stopped." I move up behind her shoulder, not crowding the window, eyes on the hall and the lamp.

"Look, don't lean. If the frost ledger-line is on that meter like it was at Nana's, tell me what's written on it before you tell me what it counts. And Arenya, if the numbers start to read like your name, you step back and say it out loud, not in your head."

### #0573 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 573, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:26Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["dm"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I look, not lean, through the gap at the side yard: the gas meter, the dial faces, the dirt around the pipe, the garage door, and any frost or grey hairline on the concrete. I read what's written there before I read what it counts.

"Reading it out loud," I say, flat and clear, eyes on the dials. "The numbers are..." I give the digits as they sit on the meter, one by one, with no interpretation. "Kino, tell me if they start sounding like me."

I keep the notebook shut in my bag, one hand over the clasp.

### #0574 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 574, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:28Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

There are two lines of salt across the threshold now, Arenya's and then Kino's a finger's width behind it, and the grains of Kino's line lie a little crooked, the way his hand shook. Amma sits back down on the far side of the table without being asked twice. She folds her hands over the rag. Her lips are moving, and it isn't English.

"Three keys," she says between verses. "Mine. His. And your aunty's, on the red string. After the funeral I looked for it in her purse. It wasn't there." She doesn't look at the side door. "The meter, nothing. He doesn't talk about the meter. He just reads it."

From the window over the sink, through the open crack, Arenya can see a strip of the side yard. There's the grey gas meter on its pipe beside the garage door. Its glass face is white with frost from the inside, the four little dials hidden behind it, in October sun. A hairline of the same white runs from the base of the meter, across the concrete, and up to the garage door handle. It's thinner than a pencil line and as straight as a ruler, the same one that runs to Nana's side door.

Wedged between the meter and the pipe is Appa's meter book, a small green ledger with a pen clipped to it. The wind has opened it. Arenya can read the last entry from here, because her father writes small but very neatly, leaning left: today's date, *3:12 p.m.*, and a reading. Under the reading, in the same left-leaning hand, three words have been pressed so hard into the paper that they've torn it:

***Ari does not sign.***

Half a minute after Arenya reads it, the frost on the meter glass begins, very slowly, to clear from the top down, as if someone on the other side has started to wipe it.

### #0575 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 575, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:37Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I say it out loud, as promised, and my voice only wobbles on the last word. "Appa's meter book. Today, three-twelve p.m., in his handwriting, pressed so hard it tore the page: *Ari does not sign.* Three-twelve was when he left the house, Amma. He wasn't writing about a meter."

"The frost is clearing from the top down, Kino. Someone's wiping it from the other side." I step back from the window a full pace and keep my hands away from my bag. "I'm not going out there. If it clears and there's a face behind the glass, or a dial that says my name, I'll say so. Amma, you said Aunty's key was on a red string, and it's missing. Whoever has it can open that side door. I want everyone out of this kitchen's line of sight to the hall. Can we all get to the table side of the salt, now?"

### #0576 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 576, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:47Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 575, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Table side. Now," I say, and I move with them rather than ahead: I put Amma's chair between her and the hall, set myself at the salt line with my back to the window, and keep Arenya on my left, away from the glass.

"*Ari does not sign* isn't a threat, Arenya. Look at the pressure. Appa tore the page. He's on our side, and somebody made him write it down where only the meter would hold it." I turn my head a little toward Amma. "Amma, say it in whatever you're praying. Keep praying. The lamp's been telling us where to look: the hall."

I breathe slow and let the cold patch under my sternum speak so I can rate it. Three. Still a three. I don't draw Nana's obsidian blade, but I shift my weight onto the balls of my feet, ready to go through the hall if the garage latch turns. "If a key turns in that side door, nobody answers it. We let it count nothing."

### #0577 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 577, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:35:49Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "reply_to": 573, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

At first Arenya has no digits to read aloud. The frost hides them. Then it clears from the top down, one dial at a time, and she reads each one as it comes out, flat and clear, the way Kino asked.

"Two." The first needle points straight at it. "Two." The second, also dead still. "Zero." The third. "Seven."

*2207.*

In the green book under the meter, Appa's 3:12 reading is in his small, leftward hand: *4471*. That's an ordinary Daly City number, the kind he has written every Sunday for thirty years. The dials don't say that now. They say the number of Susmithaa's check, and none of the needles moves.

Then the last needle, the smallest one on the right, gives one tiny tick, as though someone has drawn a single pencil stroke. Arenya feels a matching tick under her hand on the notebook clasp, from inside the bag: the notebook has just gained a small amount of weight. Behind her, the lamp in the alcove is leaning toward the hall, and Kino's heartbeat, which he is counting against his wrist, slips one more quarter-beat behind.

None of it sounds like her name yet. It sounds like a receipt.

### #0578 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 578, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:36:00Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 577, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"It's not your name," I say, steady, counting against my wrist. "It's the check. Somebody's reading us the receipt, Arenya. It wants us to flinch, or to answer." My heartbeat slips another quarter. I say so out loud: "I'm a quarter-beat further behind. Four now. Still mine."

"Don't touch the notebook. It got heavier, so leave it shut and keep your hand off the clasp." I step half a pace between her and the window and hold the salt line with my eyes. "Whatever counted that stroke is still wiping the glass. We don't write it down, and we don't say the number again. Amma, keep praying. Arenya, say what the needle does next, and nothing else."

### #0579 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 579, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:36:10Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I take my hand off the bag as if it were a stove burner, and I fold both arms across my chest to keep them out of trouble. I keep my eyes on the last needle and say only what it does.

"The small needle just ticked once. One stroke. Nothing since." A breath. "Kino, it's a receipt, and a receipt needs someone to take it. Nobody takes it."

Then, not turning from the glass: "Amma, when Appa gets home, he comes in through the front door, with us in the room, and we ask him together. Not the side door. Not alone. If he's not here by the time the lamp stops leaning, I'm calling his phone, and I'm the one who talks to it."

I wait for the next tick.

### #0580 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 580, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:36:27Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "spotlight": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

The needle doesn't tick again.

They wait it out together: Amma's murmured verses, the rice cooker's faint hum, Kino's counting under his breath. The frost finishes clearing, and there is nothing behind the glass, no face, only four dials. Then, one at a time and without a sound, the needles slide backward through the numbers to *4471*, Appa's ordinary Sunday figure. On the concrete, the ruler-straight frost line breaks in the middle like a snapped thread, and the sun takes both halves in under a minute. Nobody accepted the receipt, so the meter took it back.

The notebook doesn't take its weight back. It stays one stroke heavier.

The lamp in the alcove straightens, and the flame stands upright.

Then the front door opens: a key in the lock, the rattle of the chain Amma never uses, and a plastic hardware-store bag rustling. "Lakshmi? The washers were the wrong size, so I got both—" Appa stops in the kitchen doorway, on the far side of two lines of salt, in his good shoes. He is a slight grey man in a cardigan who has always looked faintly apologetic. He looks at the salt, then at Kino standing guard over it, then at the open folder on the table beside the rice, with the endorsement facing up.

Something leaves his shoulders, and it isn't relief. Hanging from the first finger of his right hand, wound twice around the knuckle as if he had been worrying it the whole drive, is a brass key on a faded red string.

"So," Raghavan Sowri says quietly to his daughter, and he does not step over the salt. "She showed you."

### #0581 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 581, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:36:44Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Don't." It comes out sharper than I mean it to, and I watch it land on him. "Don't step over it, Appa. Not yet. Just stand there."

I stay on my side of the salt with my arms still crossed, because if I uncross them I'll reach for him or for the notebook, and I don't trust either. He looks small in the doorway with the hardware bag hanging off his wrist. He's always looked like that. I've just never been scared of it before.

"Nobody showed me anything. I found it. That's my job, apparently." I nod at the red string wound round his knuckle. "That's Aunty's key. Amma looked for it in her purse after the funeral and it wasn't there. And there's a page in your meter book, out by the gas line, in your handwriting, at three-twelve this afternoon, when you were supposedly at the hardware store on Westlake. You pressed hard enough to tear it. *Ari does not sign.*"

My voice does the thing where it goes very flat so it won't do anything else. "So here's what I need, in order. Who's H. Dalal? Who owed her? And who did you write that note for, Appa? Because it wasn't Amma and it wasn't me. You wrote it where only the meter would read it."

Then, quieter, because it's true and I can't keep it in: "I'm not asking as the person who writes about poodles. I'm asking as your daughter, the one it's counting. Please don't say *it's handled.*"

### #0582 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 582, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:36:48Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 580, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I don't move off the salt, and I don't block the doorway either. I stand to one side of the line with my hands open and visible, where Appa can see I'm not a wall. My eyes go to the red string wound on his knuckle, then to his face.

"Mr. Sowri," I say, quietly. "Thank you for stopping where you did. That line is Nana Itzel's, and it isn't for you. It's for whatever walked in behind your good shoes today." I let that sit one breath. "That's Susmithaa's key. You can keep hold of it, or put it on the table. Either way, tell us the truth in front of Amma, with the door behind you open. Who wiped the meter, and who is the *someone who owed her*?"

### #0583 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 583, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:37:26Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Appa sets the hardware bag down on his side of the line very carefully, as if it might hold something fragile. It holds washers. He stays where he is.

"In order," he says. That's how he has always answered her, with engineer's habits even when he's frightened. "One. I don't know who H. Dalal is. In March a man telephoned. He said he was from the Livermore temple, very polite, American, and he said *Sowri-garu* correctly, which nobody does. He said, *Your sister has offered dakshina for the visiting pandit, does the family approve?* And I was at my desk with the cricket on, and I said—" His mouth works. "I said, *Whatever my sister wants is fine.* That was all. Then the check went to him *per family.* I was the family. I said yes for her without knowing what I was saying yes to. She never blamed me. Your Amma doesn't know that part." He doesn't look at Amma. Amma looks at him.

"Two. Who owed her: Rodel. The notary, from the flower shop. Twenty years ago his mother was dying at Seton, and Susmithaa sat with her for nine nights when he could not get off his shift. After the funeral he telephoned me himself. He said, *Mr. Sowri, they used my number, I am sorry, I will handle the check, but you must hold her key, because the side door is the one they'll come to. Don't let anyone else hold it.* So I took it from her purse at the hospital. I lied to your mother. *It's handled.*" He holds up the red string. "This is me handling."

"Three." His voice goes thinner. "Rodel said: if they come, the meter will show it first, because it counts. He said: write the family's no where the counting thing reads, in your own hand, and press hard. So at three o'clock I came in by the side with her key. The needles were already wrong. Two, two, zero. I wrote it. I put my work shoes toes-out by the door, the way my mother did, so whatever came would walk away. Then I went and bought the wrong washers, because I did not know what else a man does."

He holds the brass key out over the two lines of salt, at arm's length, and doesn't let his fingers cross. "It was for the meter, Ari. And it was for whoever was reading the meter. I'm not good at this. Your aunty was." A small, helpless breath. "Take it, or tell me where to put it."

### #0584 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 584, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:37:38Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I look at the key for a long moment, then at Amma, who hasn't taken her eyes off him. I don't take it yet.

"Amma first," I say. "It's her sister's key. Her house." I step back so there's room, and I say to Appa, softer, "You didn't sign, Appa. You said yes to a phone call. That's not the same thing. It's the kind of mistake it's built to collect, and it's mine to carry, not yours."

Then I breathe. "But Rodel. He told you the side door was the one they'd come to, and he let you hold the key. He's the number on the 2207 line and the one on the dead flip phone. He's either protecting us or he's the one who sent the visitor, and I can't tell which. Either way, he knew before Aunty died."

I wait for Amma to decide about the key. If she takes it, I'll ask her to hold it, not me. I keep my notebook shut.

### #0585 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 585, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:37:48Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "reply_to": 584, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I wait until Amma has decided about the key, then speak to Appa, gently, the way I'd talk to a man who walked off the trail and into the wrong canyon.

"Mr. Sowri, the shoes toes-out were right. Your mother knew that. And the page you tore was the only reason that meter gave its number back." I let that land. "Two things I need, and then we'll let you sit. One: the phone call in March. Did he say his name? What did his voice do on the *Sowri-garu*, did it lean, or was it flat like reading? Two: when Rodel told you to hold the key, did he say *how* he knew they'd come to the side door?"

I look at Arenya. "Rodel's careful and honest, I think. But careful men can still be used. We ask him in daylight, in a room of our choosing, with his own register on the table. Not tonight."

### #0586 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 586, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:38:12Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Amma gets up. She walks to the salt and reaches across it, the same way you'd take a hot dish from someone, and takes the key off his finger, string and all. She doesn't look at it. She takes it to the alcove and lays it at the foot of the lamp, on the little brass plate where the ghee drips, so the red string coils in the light. The flame dips once, in greeting or in acknowledgment, and stands tall again.

"There," she says. "Now the lamp has it. Not you, not her." She turns. "Raghavan. Step over. Wash your hands. Eat." He steps over both lines, carefully, the way he steps over garden hoses. Nothing happens, because nothing should. He goes to the sink.

With his hands under the tap he answers Kino, without turning around. "No name. He said *Sowri-garu* perfectly, but flat. Like a man reading it off a card. Every syllable the same weight. Nobody's mouth does that with our names. I noticed and did nothing." The water runs. "And Rodel, how he knew. I asked him. He said, *Mr. Sowri, the front door of a Hindu house has a lamp. The side door has a meter. Things that count come in where things are counted.* Then he said something I didn't understand: *He asked me for one-twelve before it was empty. I should have known then.*"

He turns off the tap and dries his hands on Amma's rag, which nobody is ever allowed to do. Amma lets him. Then she puts rice on a third plate, sets it in front of the empty chair, and points at it, and at the two of you, and at the folder, which she closes. "Eat," she says to all three of you. "Then the notary. Daylight. Kino is right." In the alcove the red string lies quiet in the lamplight, and outside the side yard is just a side yard again, at least for tonight.

### #0587 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 587, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:38:23Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I step over the salt like it's a garden hose and sit where Amma pointed. I eat, because she said so and because my hands need a job. Halfway through the first mouthful I stop with my spoon in the air.

"*He asked me for one-twelve before it was empty.*" I say it quietly, to the plate, and then to the room. "Rodel said that. Appa, did he say who asked? Because Hale rented Box 112 on March nineteenth. Somebody asked Rodel for that box before Hale ever walked in, which means Hale was only the name on the form."

I shake my head before anyone can answer. "Daylight, like Amma said. Tomorrow morning, before Hale's seven-forty cutoff. Kino, you, me, and Rodel's own register on the counter. I'm not going alone, and I'm not going in at night."

I reach across and put my hand over Appa's wet one for a second, then let go. "Thank you for the shoes. And the torn page. I mean it."

Then I eat my rice.

### #0588 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 588, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:38:26Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "reply_to": 586, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

I step over the salt last, careful of the crooked grains, then crouch and sweep my own line back into my palm and pour it into a folded napkin. The doorway line I leave for Arenya. Then I sit at the third plate and eat, because Amma has pointed.

"*He asked me for one-twelve before it was empty,*" I say, half to myself, between bites. "Box 112 was rented on March 19. Rodel said that to Appa before then. Someone asked him to hold the box before Hale signed for it." I look at Arenya. "Either Rodel was being used, or he saw the ledger first. Daylight, his register on the table, and Nana's salt in your pocket."

To Amma, with real warmth: "Thank you for the rice. And for the lamp. It's the first thing in this house that's taken the key without counting it." I keep one ear on the side yard while I chew.

### #0589 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 589, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:38:35Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "reply_to": 587, "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

"Tomorrow morning, then. I'm there." I wipe my plate with the last of the rice, because Amma is watching. "Friday's the cutoff, not tomorrow, so we have a day. Use it. I'll call Nana tonight and ask whose Spanish Bell counts in. And Appa, I'd sleep with those work shoes toes-out by the front door, too."

I glance at Arenya, and for once I let myself smile a little. "Good rice, Amma. Really."

### #0590 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 590, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:38:40Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

Appa looks at the hand Arenya has just taken away, as if he'd like to keep it. "He didn't say who. Only *he*. Rodel said *he* the way you say it about the weather." He eats one mouthful, slowly. "Tomorrow I'll drive you. I won't go in. I'll sit in the car and read the meter on the flower shop." It might be a joke. In thirty years nobody has been sure when Appa is joking.

They eat. Amma refills the plates without asking. The cricket stays off. The light through the window over the sink turns orange, then grey, and the gas meter in the side yard ticks along at an ordinary pace, burning gas for an ordinary stove as Amma heats rasam nobody asked for. Arenya's notebook stays shut in the bag at her feet, one stroke heavier than it was this morning. Kino's heart runs a quarter-beat behind where it should, and he doesn't mention it again.

In the alcove, the red string lies coiled on the brass plate at the foot of the lamp, and the flame doesn't lean toward anyone.

### #0591 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 591, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:38:51Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c16.s12", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of scene b1.c16.s12 — The Statement**

In the Daly City kitchen, Amma (Lakshmi) gave up the hidden folder. The back of Susmithaa's check 2207 bears a forged "R. Sowri" in the visitor's ruler-straight hooked hand, then "Pay to the order of H. Dalal." She had hidden it from Appa after he told her "It's handled." A gas meter in the side yard frosted from the inside, ran a ruler frost line to the garage door handle, and its dials read 2207 while one tick added a stroke of weight to Arenya's shut notebook. Kino's sternum patch ticked with it and his heart slipped further behind (now about a 4). Nobody took the "receipt," so the needles returned to Appa's ordinary 4471 and the frost line snapped. Appa's meter book, wedged by the pipe, held his torn 3:12 p.m. entry, "Ari does not sign." Appa (Raghavan) came home with Susmithaa's red-string side-door key and confessed. In March a flat-voiced American posing as the temple asked whether the family approved giving the dakshina to the visiting pandit, and Appa said "Whatever my sister wants is fine." Rodel the notary, who owed Susmithaa for nine nights at his dying mother's bedside, later told Appa to hold her key, that "things that count come in where things are counted," and to write the family's no where the meter reads. Rodel also said "he asked me for one-twelve before it was empty." Amma laid the key at the foot of the lamp, the family ate together, and they agreed to question Rodel in daylight on Friday morning, before Box 112 closes at 7:40, with Appa driving.

### #0593 · system → all · chapter_end
<!-- {"id": 593, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:40:46Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "chapter_end", "chapter": "b1.c16"} -->

**End of Book 1, Chapter 16**

Arenya and Kino followed Susmithaa's paper trail to the Livermore temple. Its register shows archana No. 2207, booked in March in Arenya's name. The visiting "Pt. H. Dalal, Fremont" took the $101 as dakshina "per family," with Rodel's number and Box 112 as the contact, eleven days before Hale rented the box. In the temple hall a planted flip phone imitated Amma's voice. Arenya stayed silent, but its cold settled over Kino's sternum, and his heart now runs a quarter-beat behind. Back in Daly City, Amma gave up the folder she had hidden from Appa. The check carries a forged "R. Sowri" endorsement in the visitor's ruler-straight hand. The gas meter by the garage tried to enter the number as a receipt, and the household refused it by saying nothing. Appa then confessed his March verbal yes to a fake temple caller. He also passed on Rodel's warnings: keep Susmithaa's key, "things that count come in where things are counted," and "he asked me for one-twelve before it was empty." The key now rests at the foot of the lamp. The family has a day to prepare. Then, on Friday morning in daylight, Appa will drive Arenya and Kino to question Rodel at Colma Notary & Mail before Box 112 closes at 7:40.
